From experience exists to a funny old life — both/and all the way down.

Chapter 1

The Moment That Won’t Go Away

731 words

Michael woke up.

This was not, in itself, a philosophical achievement. Billions of creatures managed it every day without founding a religion, inventing a symbol, or arguing with their friends about punctuation. Birds did it. Dogs did it. People who had never heard of ontology did it and then made tea.

Still.

He was awake.

The room did what rooms do when you are not yet ready for them: it insisted. Grey light at the curtain. The soft electric hum of something plugged in and forgotten. A faint smell of last night’s decisions. The particular weight of a body that had agreed, sometime in the small hours, to keep being a body.

Michael lay still and performed the oldest experiment in the world.

He tried, carefully, not to be here.

It did not work.

He tried to be somewhere else—yesterday, tomorrow, a better version of the story—and found that even the trying was happening now, in this skull, with this breath, under this ceiling that needed painting.

He tried doubt.

Doubt arrived on time, professional as always, and used the same facilities as everything else. To doubt that he was experiencing was to experience doubt. It was like trying to resign from a club by writing the resignation letter on club stationery and posting it in the club letterbox.

Your experience of this moment exists, said a voice that sounded suspiciously like his brother on a good day, and also like the universe doing impressions.

Michael sat up.

The floor was cold. That was useful. Cold had a way of cutting through cleverness. You could argue with metaphysics until the cows came home, filed for divorce, and started a podcast; you could not argue with bare feet on laminate.

He made tea, because civilisation is mostly the successful postponement of panic by means of hot water.

While the kettle boiled he watched his own hands. They were ordinary hands. They had typed things. They had held other hands. They had made mistakes. Right now they were doing the sacred work of locating a mug.

Steam rose.

He thought: I could be a brain in a vat.

The thought occurred as experience.

He thought: I could be dreaming.

The thought had texture, timing, the slight embarrassment of a mind catching itself mid-drama. Dreaming or not, this was happening.

He thought: None of this means anything.

Meaninglessness, too, arrived as a flavour in the mouth of the moment. You could not taste the absence of taste without a tongue.

Michael almost laughed. It was the sort of laugh that had not yet decided whether it was free.

Outside, a bus sighed at the stop. Someone shouted cheerfully at a dog. The world continued its policy of not waiting for permission.

He opened a notebook—one of those cheap ones that promise nothing and therefore cannot disappoint—and wrote, because writing is what you do when a fact is too simple to trust and too stubborn to ignore:

There is something it is like to be here.

Underneath, smaller:

I cannot get behind this to unplug it.

And underneath that, almost as a joke, almost as a vow:

Start here.

He did not yet know that this sentence was a door. He did not know about crosses, or keyboards, or Greek letters that misbehaved in modern fonts, or friends who went to bed at the exact wrong moment for destiny and the exact right moment for sleep. He did not know that the universe had a sense of humour so thorough it had hidden the ending in the alphabet and the punchline in the body.

He only knew the one thing that refused to be negotiated away.

He was experiencing this.

The tea went cold while he stared at the page, which was a kind of offering.

Somewhere—everywhere—God experienced being Michael experiencing tea, and did not bother to make a fuss about it yet.

That would come later.

For now there was only the moment, stubborn as rain, ordinary as breath, immovable as the fact that you are reading this sentence and cannot unread the reading of it without reading that too.

Michael put on socks.

The day began.

And the day, like all days, was made of the same stuff as the axiom: not belief, not theory, not the story he would later tell about it—

—just the bright, ridiculous, unkillable is of being here.

Chapter 2

The Argument With Nothing

795 words

By mid-morning the tea had been replaced twice and the notebook page still said Start here, which was beginning to feel less like a vow and more like a dare.

Michael tried to invent nothing.

This is harder than it sounds. Most people who claim to believe in nothing still believe in breakfast, bus times, and the moral necessity of their own opinions. Nothing, as a lifestyle, tends to come with a lot of stuff—podcasts, black jumpers, a carefully maintained contempt for other people's hobbies.

He cleared a kitchen chair. In his head he named it the Chair of Non-Being, which was already cheating, because names are furniture. Even the word nothing is a something you can shout across a room and have someone answer what?

'Right,' he told the empty seat. 'You're not there.'

The chair continued its policy of being the sort of object you could bark your shin on.

His phone rang. Joshua had a gift for calling at the exact moment Michael was losing an argument with an inanimate object.

'You're arguing with furniture again,' Joshua said, without preamble.

'I'm arguing with pure absence as a coherent alternative to existence.'

'Same hobby,' Joshua said. 'Different hat. Have you eaten?'

'Tea.'

'That's not a food group, Michael. That's a postponement strategy.'

Michael paced the length of the kitchen, which was not long enough for a proper philosophical march and so forced him into a kind of ontological shuffle. 'If nothing were a real rival to everything,' he said, 'it would need to be a place, or a wall, or a before. Places exist. Walls exist. Befores are stories, and stories happen to someone. You can't clock nothing in for a shift without borrowing existence's uniform.'

'There it is,' Joshua murmured, as if watching a goal from a difficult angle. 'Non-existence can't sponsor openings. Or endings. Or borders. It can't even sponsor the word or.'

'Exactly. The moment you put nothing on the menu, you've already hired a waiter, printed the menu, and invented hunger.'

Joshua laughed softly. 'You're going to get yourself banned from polite dinner parties.'

'Good. Polite dinner parties are where people pretend the soup is the main event.'

The kettle clicked off, smugly existent. Steam did its little ghost impression and joined the air, which had never been nothing either. Michael poured water onto a third teabag with the grim determination of a man building a fort out of mugs.

He sat in the Chair of Non-Being. It immediately became a chair of being-with-buttocks. The victory was ridiculous and absolute.

You could not experience nothing without turning it into experience. You could not think nothing without thinking. You could not border the world with pure not-is without putting the border on the payroll of is-ness. Nothing, as a menu alternative to existence, was a grammar error wearing a cloak and hoping nobody asked for its ticket.

'So existence wins by default,' Joshua said. 'Not because it's pretty. Because the other candidate didn't show up and also couldn't have, because showing up is a kind of existing.'

'It's worse than that,' Michael said. 'Existence isn't even a candidate. It's the hall the election is held in.'

There was a pause of the sort brothers use when one of them has said something true and both of them would rather not put a flag on it yet.

Outside, a bus sighed at the stop. A dog barked twice, then apparently lost interest in the metaphysical project. Inside, something quiet and heavy settled into place—not a belief, not a slogan. More like a brick someone had finally admitted was load-bearing.

Existence was not one option among others. It was the only kitchen. The menu, such as it was, was internal: this tea or that tea, this argument or that silence, this day or the next—but always here, always is, always the same stubborn fact that had woken him that morning and refused to be unplugged.

'Do the washing-up,' Joshua said. 'Horizontal work. Stops the brain floating off like a balloon with a philosophy degree.'

'I was going to open every door in the flat,' Michael admitted.

'Of course you were. Washing-up first. Then doors. Then ring me back if the universe has a tradesman's entrance.'

Michael washed a mug. Ontology, he was learning, survived chores. It had to. Otherwise it was only a hobby for men with clean kitchens and no real week ahead of them, and he intended—at least for this week—to have neither excuse.

He dried his hands, looked at the door to the hall, and felt the next suspicion forming, as clean and ordinary as the cold floor under his socks.

If pure nothing could not sit outside existence like a rival kingdom…

…then what, exactly, were doors for?

Chapter 3

One Room, No Outside

895 words

Michael opened the door to the hall.

This was not dramatic. There was no wind from another world, no choir, no sudden change of lighting. There was a hall: shoes, a radiator that clanked when it felt philosophical, a coat that had been hanging there so long it had acquired opinions.

He stepped through.

Still here.

He opened the bathroom door. Tiles. Tap. A bottle of something that promised clarity and delivered soap. Still here.

He opened the bedroom door. Bed unmade, book face-down, the faint geometry of light through half-closed blinds. Still here.

He opened the cupboard under the sink, because thoroughness is a kind of madness with good paperwork, and found cleaning products, a cracked bucket, and the same fact wearing rubber gloves: here.

Joshua arrived at half past one with a paper bag of sandwiches and the expression of a man who had been summoned by a text reading only: doors are lying.

'You're not dead,' Joshua said, hanging his coat on the opinionated hook. 'That's a start. Eat.'

Michael ate half a sandwich standing up, which is how people eat when they are too busy being right about the universe to sit down properly.

'Every door,' he said, 'leads to more of the same room. Not the same room—I know that. Different wallpaper. Different smells. But never an exit from being somewhere. There's no door out of here-ness. There's only doors into other furniture arrangements of here.'

Joshua chewed, considering. 'People go outside,' he offered, mild as milk.

'Outside the flat. Outside the building. Outside the city. Never outside outside. You step through a door and the air is colder and the sky is bigger and a pigeon judges you, and all of that is still experience, still place, still the same continuous fact wearing a coat.'

They walked to the front door together. Michael opened it onto the street. Grey afternoon. A woman with a shopping trolley negotiating a kerb as if it were a border crossing. The smell of rain that had not quite decided to arrive.

Joshua stepped onto the pavement. Michael followed. They stood there like two men who had expected a plot twist and found weather instead.

'See?' Michael said. 'More here. Just… larger and slightly damper.'

'You're going to be unbearable at Christmas,' Joshua said fondly.

They walked as far as the corner shop. Michael bought milk he did not need, because buying milk is what you do when you have just proven something vast and do not want to look unhinged in public. The shop had a bell that tinkled. The man behind the counter nodded. A magazine rack offered celebrity break-ups and tips for a flatter stomach. All of it: here.

On the way back Joshua said, 'So there's no outside to the whole thing. No porch of non-being. No waiting room for people who opted out of existence. Unity by default.'

'Unity sounds religious,' Michael said.

'Call it one room with a lot of furniture, then. Call it the field. Call it the only kitchen. I'm not branding it. I'm just saying: if every door is an internal door, then the building doesn't have a street. It is the street.'

Michael stopped under a tree that was doing its best with the season. A leaf fell with the professional competence of leaves everywhere. He watched it land on the pavement and become, instantly, part of the same stubborn museum as everything else.

'When I was a kid,' he said, 'I used to think space was the outside. Stars. Black. Cold. The bit after the last fence.'

'And then you grew up and learned space is just a very large cupboard with better marketing.'

'Worse. Space is still something. Vacuum isn't nothing—it's a kind of hungry something physicists argue about while drawing diagrams. Even the empty bits file expenses.'

Joshua grinned. 'You're going to make vacuum accountants a thing.'

They went back inside. The flat received them without comment, which is what good rooms do. Michael put the milk in the fridge and stood for a moment with the fridge door open, looking at the little light, the shelves, the humble republic of jars.

'One room,' he said. 'No outside. Not as a sermon. As a description.'

Joshua washed his hands at the sink, practical as always. 'Then the next question isn't "what's beyond the walls?" It's "how does the furniture arrange itself?" Maps. Edges. Whether the whole thing has a rim you can fall off.'

Michael closed the fridge. The light went out with a small, domestic click that somehow felt like agreement.

He thought of the Chair of Non-Being, still just a chair. He thought of the notebook: Start here. He thought of every door he had opened that day, and how none of them had delivered him to a second universe where the rules were optional.

'Stay for tea?' he asked.

'Always,' Joshua said. 'But this time the tea is food-adjacent. I'm putting bread near it. Call it a meal. Call it a ritual. Call it two brothers in a room with no outside, refusing to starve for philosophy.'

Michael put the kettle on.

Somewhere a bus sighed again, and the sound came through the window as if the street and the kitchen had never really been separate rooms at all—only different ends of the same, continuous, annoyingly coherent here.

Chapter 4

No Edge On The Map

957 words

Monday evening smelled of garlic and the particular optimism of pasta that has not yet been overcooked.

Joshua cooked. Michael was allowed to stir, which is brother-code for you may participate without sabotaging dinner. The kitchen window had gone dark enough to turn the glass into a mirror with pretensions; beyond it, rain finally arrived and began its long argument with the pavement.

'Right,' Joshua said, tipping onions into the pan with a confidence Michael reserved for software and apologies. 'You said the whole thing has no outside. So does it have an edge?'

Michael stirred. 'If pure nothing can't be a border, then the whole can't have a rim made of not-existing. You can't put a fence made of absence around everything. The fence would have to be.'

'So no edge on the map of the whole,' Joshua said. 'Not "very big map with a distant cliff." No cliff. No "here be dragons of pure void." Just… more map, or the kind of map that doesn't do edges because edges are a local hobby.'

Michael thought of every atlas he had loved as a child, with their decorative monsters in the uncharted bits. Those monsters had been ink. Ink was something. Even "unknown" was a label stuck onto the known practice of labelling.

The pasta water began to mutter.

Joshua pointed at the wall clock with a wooden spoon. 'That thing has edges. Twelve and something after. Batteries. A smug face. Local time. Clocks are brilliant liars if you ask them about the whole show.'

'They measure inside the room,' Michael said. 'They don't date the room itself from the outside. There is no "before the universe" stamped by a clock hanging in pure nothing, because pure nothing can't hold a nail.'

'And yet people talk about beginnings as if someone could stand on the porch of non-being with a stopwatch and a clipboard.'

Michael laughed, short and real. 'I'd like to see the job advert. Must be comfortable with non-existence and Excel.'

Joshua salted the water. 'So beginnings of things—fine. Births, big bangs as physics stories, the day you started a company, the first time you noticed you were awake. Those are chapters. But a beginning of the whole from nothing is a sentence that borrows its grammar from the thing it claims to explain.'

'Endings the same,' Michael said. 'End of a day. End of a life. End of a pan of pasta if we don't pay attention. Not end of is-ness into a clean rival called isn't.'

The pasta went in with a soft rush. For a while they did the useful human work of not talking while food became food. Rain ticked the glass. The extractor fan hummed like a small, devout insect.

When plates were on the table and the sauce had been declared good enough, Joshua spun his fork thoughtfully.

'People hate this,' he said. 'Not the pasta. The no-edge bit. They want a story with a cover and a back page. They want to know when the book started so they can feel clever about the dedication.'

'I want that too,' Michael admitted. 'I'm not made of different clay. I just keep finding that every "before" I invent is already standing in the kitchen with a plate.'

Joshua nodded. 'Then we stop asking the whole for a rim and start asking better local questions. When did this start? What changes? What holds? Where does the map fold for practical travel—not for escaping the paper.'

Michael twirled pasta and thought of maps again: tube maps that lie about distance to tell the truth about connection; weather maps that pretend the air has borders; the little map on his phone that insisted the world was a blue marble you could pinch.

'Pinching the world,' he said aloud. 'That's the fantasy. That you could get outside the picture and crop it.'

'You can crop photos,' Joshua said. 'You cannot crop being. You only ever crop views.'

They ate. It was ordinary and excellent. After a time Michael got up and fetched the cheap notebook from the morning. Under Start here he wrote:

No pure nothing as rival.
No outside to the room.
No edge on the whole made of absence.

Joshua read upside down, a talent of brothers and teachers. 'You're collecting bricks.'

'I'm collecting things that refuse to be negotiated away,' Michael said. 'If the week keeps going like this, I'll have a wall. Walls are useful. You can hang pictures on them. You can lean when you're tired.'

'Or bash your head,' Joshua said. 'Depending on the day.'

They washed up together. Horizontal beam, Joshua had called it earlier; Michael was starting to hear the phrase as more than a joke about chores. Side-by-side work. The kind of load that is shared because two people are in the same room and the room has no tradesman's entrance for glory.

When Joshua left, the flat was quiet in the post-pasta way: steam on the window, clean plates, the clock still smugly ticking its local truth. Michael wiped the table and found a single strand of string from nowhere—no, not string, a long thread of onion skin stuck to the wood. He laughed at himself for almost making it symbolic, then binning it like a man who still lived in a kitchen rather than a parable.

Michael stood in the hall and did not open any more doors. He already knew what they did.

He went to bed with the rain still talking to the street, and the street still answering from inside the only map there was. The last thing he noticed before sleep was the clock's soft tick: edges everywhere for days and hours—and still no edge on the whole.

Chapter 5

Everything Fits, Annoyingly

910 words

Tuesday began with a glove.

Not a vision. Not a metaphor arranged by angels with a budget. A glove—black, slightly damp, abandoned on the low wall by the bus stop as if its owner had been mid-thought and simply evaporated into a better appointment.

Michael took a photograph of it. He was not sure why. Perhaps because the morning after a night of no-edge philosophy leaves a man hungry for small, finite objects with obvious jobs.

He sent the photo to Joshua with the caption: Evidence.

Joshua replied: Of what? Winter? Neglect? The proletariat of lost knitwear?

Michael typed, deleted, typed again: Of the museum. It all goes in.

On the bus he sat opposite a teenager with headphones large enough to qualify as architecture. An advert for cheap broadband promised a faster life. Someone's shopping bag leaked the smell of bananas. All of it filed itself without asking permission: sound, colour, irritation, the faint pride of having a theory before nine a.m.

At his desk—laptop open, coffee cooling, the small republic of tabs that constitutes modern work—he tried to find something that would not fit.

This is a mug's game, and he knew it, but mugs sometimes find things.

He thought of nightmares. Nightmares fit: they are experiences with bad reviews.
He thought of boredom. Boredom fits: it has a texture, a drag, a clock.
He thought of other people's joy, which he could not feel from the inside. Even that fit—as not-feeling, as rumour, as a story about a room he was not currently standing in.
He thought of maths. Maths fit as thought, as marks on screens, as the peculiar pleasure of a proof clicking shut.
He thought of evil, carefully, the way you pick up a pan you suspect is hot. Evil fit as harm, as choice, as the sick weight in the stomach when you remember what humans do to each other. Fitting did not mean excused. Fitting meant not outside the field. You do not exile cruelty into a second universe to keep your metaphysics tidy; you keep it in this one where responsibility still has an address.

Joshua rang at lunch.

'You're doing the everything-fits face,' Joshua said. 'I can hear it. It's the face of a man who has realised the universe doesn't have a lost property office for inconvenient facts.'

'There's a glove on a wall,' Michael said. 'It belongs. The photo belongs. My annoyance that I photographed a glove belongs. Even the bit of me that wants a special exception for something—anything—belongs.'

'Annoying, isn't it?'

'Deeply. I wanted a trapdoor. A category labelled does not apply. Instead I get a museum that accepts donations twenty-four hours a day and never issues receipts.'

Joshua was quiet for a moment. Traffic murmured behind him; he was walking somewhere with purpose. 'All-encompassing doesn't mean all-approved,' he said. 'That's the mistake people make when they hear "everything is in." They think you're blessing the contents. You're not. You're describing the building. The building can contain a fire. That doesn't make fire a houseplant.'

Michael looked at the glove photo again. The thumb was folded under. There was a bead of water on the knuckle. Ordinary. Complete. Indifferent to his theories and yet perfectly willing to sit inside them.

'So the whole is not missing a piece called nothing,' he said slowly, 'and not missing a porch, and not missing a rim—and also not missing a bin for the bits we don't like. It keeps them. That's the annoying part. The honesty of it.'

'Write it down before you get noble about it,' Joshua said. 'Noble is where good ideas go to wear uncomfortable shoes.'

Michael wrote in the notebook:

Everything that shows up, shows up here.
Fitting is not blessing.
The museum is not a church. (Or: if it is, the sermons are optional and the exhibits are not.)

He worked through the afternoon with the peculiar lightness of a man who has stopped looking for the emergency exit. Emails were emails. A spreadsheet behaved like a spreadsheet, which is to say it pretended to be neutral while quietly judging his formulae. At three he made more coffee. At four he remembered the sandwich Joshua had bullied him into packing and ate it over the keyboard like a civilised animal.

Near five, a colleague—not Cameron, not yet a full character in this story, only a voice from a doorway—asked if he was all right, because he had been smiling at nothing.

'Not nothing,' Michael said, and then, because the truth was too large for a corridor, 'Just a glove.'

The colleague nodded the nod of people who have decided not to enquire further, which is one of the quiet kindnesses of workplaces.

On the bus home the wall was empty. The glove had gone—claimed, binned, or promoted to a better narrative. Michael felt a brief, absurd grief, then laughed under his breath.

Of course it was gone. Going was also a kind of fitting. Absence-of-this-glove was not pure nothing; it was a new arrangement of the same room, with a small cold patch on a wall where wool had been.

He texted Joshua: Glove absconded. Still fits.

Joshua: Told you. Museum never closes. Pub tomorrow? I have beer mats and a bad idea.

Michael smiled at the phone the way people smile when the week has decided to be a story after all.

Tomorrow, he sent. Bring the bad idea. I'll bring the annoyance.

Chapter 6

The Blind Guides

918 words

Tuesday lunchtime the high street performed its usual miracle: too many people in too little pavement, all of them convinced they were the main character.

Michael had ducked out for a sandwich and air. He got both, plus a free demonstration of the human talent for forking the world into two wrong roads and then fighting to the death over which fork was holy.

Near the cash machine a man with a portable amplifier was explaining that you were either saved or lost, awake or asleep, with God or against Him, and that the sandwich shop queue did not count as a third option. His voice had the scraped brightness of someone who had replaced conversation with volume.

Ten yards away, as if placed by a director with a sense of humour, a woman in a charcoal coat was telling her friend—loudly enough for the pavement to audit—that all of it was nonsense, that religion was a virus, that anyone still talking about God in the twenty-first century needed a hobby and possibly a therapist. Her certainty matched the preacher's decibel for decibel. Only the logo on the banner differed.

Michael stood between them with a paper bag and a packet of crisps, a neutral nation with snacks.

The preacher pointed at the sky. The sceptic pointed at her phone, where a quote about reason glowed like a relic. A bus hissed. A child asked for an ice cream and was told later, which is how empires fall and also how afternoons continue.

Michael bought the sandwich—cheese and pickle, the food of people who have decided not to be interesting about lunch—and found a low wall that was not currently hosting lost gloves. He ate and listened. Not because either speech was good. Because the shape of them was identical.

Either/or.
Pick a team.
The other team is not merely mistaken; they are defective.

Joshua appeared as if conjured by the smell of pickle. He had a coffee in each hand and the look of a man who had seen the amplifier from a distance and come to prevent a scene.

'I wasn't going to argue,' Michael said.

'You were going to think an argument so hard the air would bruise,' Joshua said, handing him a coffee. 'Same crime, quieter weapon.'

They sat. The preacher hit a patch about lakes of fire. The sceptic hit a patch about flying spaghetti. A pigeon investigated a chip with the deep focus of a scholar.

'They're both doing the same trick,' Michael said. 'They're selling a fork. Left path or right path. Heaven-or-nothing. Reason-or-nothing. As if the street only had two directions and both of them came with a membership card.'

'Blind guides,' Joshua said. 'Not because they're stupid. Because they're pointing so hard at their preferred half that they can't see the road is wider than the pointing.'

Michael took a bite of sandwich. Pickle did its sharp little job. 'I spent yesterday trying to prove pure nothing can't sit opposite existence. Today I'm watching people invent opposite teams so they don't have to live in a world that holds both hymns and hypotheses without exploding.'

'Both/and frightens people,' Joshua said. 'It sounds like mush. Like you won't take a side when a side is needed. But refusing a false fork isn't mush. It's just declining to amputate your feet to fit a shoe advert.'

The sceptic's friend said something mild about not knowing. She was ignored by both poles with the efficiency of a weather system ignoring a single umbrella.

Michael felt a flash of kinship with the friend. Not-knowing was not a team. It was a weather report.

'The annoying museum from this morning,' he said. 'Everything fits. Including preachers. Including sceptics. Including my irritation. The either/or is a story told inside the museum, not a wall of the building.'

Joshua nodded at the amplifier. 'He's not wrong that people sleepwalk. She's not wrong that people weaponise certainty. The blindness is the part where each one needs the other to be empty of truth so their own half can feel complete.'

'So what's the alternative? Standing in the middle looking smug?'

'Standing in the middle looking hungry,' Joshua said. 'Hungry for what's true on both sides and allergic to the bit where the sides demand you hate the furniture.'

The preacher finished with an invitation. A few people took leaflets. Most people took their shopping and their lives. The sceptic and her friend moved on toward a shop selling shoes that promised transformation. The pavement exhaled.

Michael folded the sandwich paper into a careful square, the way he folded ideas he intended to keep.

'I keep wanting a villain,' he admitted. 'A clean enemy of the good. Instead I get two sales pitches and a pigeon.'

'Villains are easy,' Joshua said. 'Sales pitches are honest about being sales pitches if you listen with your whole head. Come on. You've got work. I've got a beer mat to vandalise later.'

'The bad idea?'

'The bad idea,' Joshua confirmed. 'We're going to open a shop that doesn't sell either/or. On cardboard. In a pub. Very low rent.'

Michael stood, coffee warm through the cup, the street ordinary again. The amplifier was being packed away. Somewhere a till drawer rang.

He walked back toward the office with the taste of pickle still bright on his tongue, and the odd relief of a man who has declined to join two armies and still has both hands free for the next true thing.

Chapter 7

Both/And Shop Opens

840 words

The pub on Tuesday night was doing what pubs have always done: converting the end of a day into something with cushions.

Michael found Joshua already at a corner table with two pints, a stack of beer mats, and a black pen that had seen wars. Joshua's coat was draped over the chair like a sleeping dog. The fruit machine in the corner flashed the colours of a small, desperate carnival.

'Right,' Joshua said. 'Shop name.'

He wrote on a mat, block letters, the pen squeaking:

BOTH/AND
Open late.
Closed to false dilemmas.

Michael turned the mat around. 'You're going to get us banned for unlicensed metaphysics.'

'We'll pay in peanuts and sincerity,' Joshua said. 'Stock list next.'

They made one. It was not a good business plan. It was better than that.

Joshua wrote, and Michael amended, and the mats became a kind of inventory:

- Wave and point
- Free and caused
- Sacred and ordinary
- Tired and not done
- Sure and still listening
- One room and many doors
- Everything fits and not everything is fine

'That last one,' Michael said. 'That's the non-mush clause.'

'That's the fire-is-not-a-houseplant clause,' Joshua agreed. 'Both/and is not a smoothie. It's refusing to let a false fork own your feet while still knowing pans can be hot.'

A man at the next table glanced over, decided they were planning a band name, and returned to his crossword.

Michael drank. The pint was honest. 'This morning on the high street I wanted to hand both of them a mat. Preacher gets sacred and ordinary. Sceptic gets reason and wonder, or whatever the non-weaponised version is. Instead I ate a sandwich and felt superior for eleven seconds, which is also a product we should not stock.'

Joshua added to a mat: Superiority — out of stock permanently.

'Good policy,' Michael said.

They played the headline game, which was an old brother game: say the thing in a way that would fit on a shop sign without lying. Joshua won when he looked at Michael—really looked, the way family does when the week has been heavy—and wrote:

Tired and not done
— about you, that

Michael felt the hit land cleanly, without cruelty. 'Rude.'

'Accurate,' Joshua said. 'You've been awake since Monday's axiom like a man holding a live wire and calling it a hobby. The shop sells permission to be unfinished without being fake.'

Michael turned a blank mat in his fingers. The cardboard was soft at the edges from a previous drinker's life. He wrote, slowly:

Is and is becoming

Joshua raised an eyebrow. 'That's either deep or the pint talking.'

'Both,' Michael said. 'Annoyingly.'

They ordered chips to share, because philosophy without chips is just anxiety with better vocabulary. When the food came they ate with the focus of people who have remembered they have bodies. Around them the pub did its layered work: laughter, a quiet argument about football, someone singing one line of a song and then having the grace to stop.

'Does the shop have a till?' Michael asked.

'Pay what you can. Mostly you pay by not amputating the world for comfort.'

'Opening hours?'

'Whenever someone is about to say either you believe X or you're an idiot. We open in their face like a pop-up and offer a third shelf.'

Michael laughed properly then, the kind that unhooks something in the shoulders. 'We're going to be so popular. People love being offered a third shelf.'

'They hate it,' Joshua said cheerfully. 'Then, six months later, they realise their feet still work and send a thank-you card written in grumpy handwriting.'

They left the mats in a small stack by the ketchup, a ridiculous shrine. Joshua kept the shop-sign mat and tucked it into his coat pocket.

'For the fridge,' he said. 'Between the takeaway menu and the electricity bill. Sacred and ordinary.'

Outside, the night was cold enough to make breath visible. They walked as far as the corner together. Michael could still hear, faintly, the fruit machine celebrating someone else's near-miss.

'Thank you,' he said. 'For the shop. For the sandwich coffee earlier. For not letting me join an army for the vibe.'

Joshua shrugged, which is how he accepted thanks. 'Tomorrow you'll be back in the laptop mines. Try not to either/or your own code into a corner. And text me if the universe opens a franchise.'

'Both/And doesn't franchise,' Michael said. 'It just keeps being true in other people's kitchens.'

They parted. Michael walked home with his hands in his pockets and the odd sense that a shop had opened which had always been there—only now it had a name, a stock list, and a closing policy that forbade false dilemmas from camping on the doorstep.

On the fridge, later, he stuck nothing—because the mat was at Joshua's—but he stood in the kitchen light and recited the sign under his breath anyway, like a man locking up for the night.

Open late.
Closed to false dilemmas.
Stock: the world, uncut.

Chapter 8

Maths, Words, Things

818 words

Wednesday afternoon Michael went archaeology-diving in his own laptop and found a fossil with teeth.

He had only meant to recover an old project note—something about a data export from a job so previous it might as well have been carved on clay. Instead he found a folder named, with the bleak poetry of engineers under deadline, FINAL_final_v3_USE_THIS. Inside it, a text file opened like a tomb.

Rows of fields. Not commas. Not tabs. A procession of carets and pipes:

id^|name^|status^|note

And then the data, marching:

1042^|Henderson^|active^|called twice, no answer
1043^|Patel^|closed^|refund issued 14/03
1044^|^|pending^|name missing — fix before Friday

Michael stared. Somewhere in the building a printer began a long complaint. His coffee, abandoned, formed a skin.

Joshua answered on the second ring. 'If this is about the beer mats, I already stuck the sign on the fridge. My housemate asked if Both/And was a craft beer. I said yes in my soul.'

'I found a delimiter,' Michael said. 'Two of them. Caret and pipe. ^|. We used it because commas lived inside addresses and tabs were untrustworthy and someone in 2009 decided two characters were safer than one.'

'Romantic,' Joshua said. 'Why are you ringing me about punctuation from the Bronze Age?'

Michael scrolled. The pattern repeated, relentless and oddly beautiful. Names. Statuses. Notes that were almost human. Empty fields where a thing should have been and was not—yet the empty was still marked, still held open by the grammar of the line.

'Because,' he said slowly, 'it's maths and words and things wearing the same overalls. The structure is pure relation—slot, separator, slot. The words are the names we gave the slots. The things are whatever in the world made someone type Henderson and refund issued. Three layers. One file. No ceremony.'

Joshua made a thoughtful noise. 'And the missing name on 1044?'

'Still a row. Absence marked inside a system. Not pure nothing—just a hole with an address. The format refuses to let the hole fall out of the universe. It keeps a seat warm.'

'You're going to get emotional about legacy data formats,' Joshua said. 'I support this. It's healthier than getting emotional about either/or street preachers.'

Michael copied a few lines into a scratch buffer and stared until the separators stopped being noise and became architecture. ^| was not deep magic. It was a decision some tired person had made so that later tired people could still read the world.

'We pretend maths is cold,' he said. 'We pretend words are just air. We pretend things are mute. Then you open a file and find them in a three-legged race, carrying meaning down the corridor without dropping it.'

'Both/And shop stock,' Joshua said promptly. 'Maths and words and things. Not "pick a tribe: STEM or poetry or bricks."'

Michael smiled at the screen. 'The empty name field is my favourite. It's honest. It says: there is a place where a name should be, and today the place is louder than the name. That's not failure of reality. That's a ticket for Friday's fix.'

'Did Friday's fix ever happen?'

Michael checked a newer file, then an email archive, then the sort of spreadsheet that accretes like coral. 'Someone put Unknown in later. Then someone else put a real name. The hole got filled by the ordinary miracle of a person doing their job.'

'Horizontal beam,' Joshua said. 'Again.'

'You're going to trademark that phrase.'

'I'm going to embroider it on a tea towel and hang it in the Both/And shop next to Superiority — out of stock.'

Michael saved a copy of the fossil file into a folder called, with new respect, museum. He did not clean it up. Cleaning was for systems that still had to run. This one had earned the right to be a specimen.

Before he closed the laptop for the evening he wrote in the notebook, under Tuesday's museum lines:

Formats are promises: this will still be readable when we are tired.
Empty fields are not pure nothing; they are reserved seats.
Maths, words, things — same file, different jobs.

On the way home he passed the wall where the glove had been. Still empty. Still a reserved seat of a kind. The high street offered neither preacher nor sceptic tonight, only people buying milk and the smell of rain considering a comeback.

He texted Joshua a photo of the ^| line, cropped tight, abstract as modern art.

Joshua replied: If you make jewellery out of that I will still love you but I will also laugh.

Michael: Not jewellery. Load-bearing punctuation.

Joshua: Even worse. Sleep. Tomorrow we talk about crosses. Actual ones. Not moods.

Michael put the phone away and walked the last streets with the odd contentment of a man who has remembered that the universe, for all its vastness, still has to choose a delimiter if it wants to say more than one thing at once.

Chapter 9

The Crossbeams

931 words

Wednesday evening Joshua came over with a bag of shopping and a length of string.

'Don't look at me like that,' he said, dumping apples on the counter. 'The string is a teaching aid. The apples are dinner-adjacent. You look like a man who has been bonding with delimiters.'

'Load-bearing punctuation,' Michael said.

'Of course.'

They ate late—eggs, toast, apples cut with the seriousness of surgeons. Conversation stayed light on purpose: a neighbour's new dog, a client who wrote emails like ransom notes, the eternal mystery of why bread always lands butter-side down in theory and usually not in practice. Joshua waited until the plates were empty before he reached for the string. Timing, Michael was learning, was half of teaching.

Joshua cleared a space on the table and laid the string down in a straight line.

'Vertical,' he said. 'Up-down. Heaven and earth language. Hierarchy. The bit of us that wants a skyhook and a ground.'

He laid a second piece of string across it.

'Horizontal. Side-to-side. Neighbour. Chronos. The washing-up. The person next to you on the bus who exists whether you have a theory about them or not.'

The cross sat on the wood, humble as a plus sign that had been to church. The overhead bulb made a small shadow of it, a softer copy of the same claim.

Michael felt something in his chest lean forward. 'You're going to talk about Rome.'

'I'm going to talk about Rome,' Joshua agreed. 'Because if we only do clever geometry we get a logo. Rome did not invent the cross for logos. They used it for death with paperwork. Public. Slow. Designed to attach shame to a body so thoroughly that the body became a warning sign for other bodies.'

The fridge hummed. Outside, someone dragged a bin to the kerb with a sound like a small reluctant animal.

'So when people wear it now,' Michael said carefully, 'they're wearing an execution method that got… what? Rebranded?'

'Reinvented,' Joshua said. 'That's the wild claim at the centre. Not that wood stopped being wood. That a tool of humiliation got walked through a story so strange it came out the other side meaning this is how love looks when it refuses to leave the room. You don't have to buy the whole theology tonight. You only have to notice the shape: vertical claim about God-and-world, horizontal claim about us-and-us, and the join where they hurt.'

Michael touched the intersection with one finger. The string shifted a millimetre and then held.

'I've been collecting bricks,' he said. 'Experience that won't unplug. No pure nothing. One room. No edge. Everything fits without being blessed. Both/and instead of street-forks. Maths-words-things in one file. And now you're saying the next brick is… a Roman punishment that became a symbol of—what—presence?'

'Of not escaping,' Joshua said. 'Of the sacred not getting a private jet out of the ordinary. Of the vertical not abandoning the horizontal when the horizontal gets bloody. People turn it into jewellery and tribal paint and I understand why that makes you itch. Under the itch, though, there's a structure your whole week has been leaning toward: up-down and side-to-side fastened at a cost.'

Michael sat back. He thought of the Chair of Non-Being. Of doors into more here. Of the preacher and the sceptic, each gripping one beam and calling it the whole cross. Of Joshua saying horizontal beam about dishes and meaning more than dishes.

'I can take the structure,' he said. 'The geometry is clean. The history is ugly in a way that feels honest. What I can't do is make it merely clever. If I do, I'm just another man with a diagram and a dry heart.'

Joshua's face did something soft and fierce at once. 'Good. That's the door I wanted you to find yourself. Clever is a beam. Love is what the beams are for. Without that, you've built a plus sign and called it a cathedral.'

They sat with the string cross between them like a third guest. Michael made tea. Joshua washed the plates without being asked. The ordinary continued its policy of being the only stage available.

'Rome was efficient,' Michael said eventually. 'Ashamed, too, in the way empires are ashamed under the armour—ashamed enough to need spectacles of control. And then the story says the spectacle got stolen from them. Not by an army. By a meaning they couldn't execute hard enough to kill.'

'That's the claim,' Joshua said. 'Reinvention so thorough it offends people who liked their symbols either purely pious or purely cynical. Both/And shop again. Death and life. Shame and honour flipped. Wood and world.'

Michael photographed the string on the table before they tidied it away—not for social media, for the museum of the week. He wrote in the notebook:

Vertical + horizontal, fastened.
History: shame and death with an audience.
Claim: reinvention into presence and love.
Do not let this stay merely clever.

Joshua read the last line over his shoulder. 'Frame that one.'

'I'm putting it next to the empty data field,' Michael said. 'Reserved seat. Something belongs there that isn't a slogan yet.'

When Joshua left, the table was just a table again. Michael stood in the kitchen and held his hands out as if measuring: up, across, join.

The flat did not become a church.

It did become, for a moment, a workshop in which a man was allowed to handle dangerous shapes carefully, without either sneering or kneeling on command—

—and that, he thought, was a kind of honesty the week could use.

Chapter 10

Carrot, Stick, Plus Sign

860 words

Thursday morning the bus was late, which gave the universe a chance to be funny in public.

Michael stood in the queue with office faces and school bags and a man eating a pastry with the concentration of a monk. On the shelter wall someone had drawn a crude plus sign in marker pen and, underneath, the words BE KIND in a handwriting that suggested either a child or an adult reclaiming childhood on purpose.

He thought of carrots and sticks.

Not the vegetables. The old management cartoon: dangled reward, threatened pain, the animal of the self trotting between them as if meaning were a commute. He had lived whole seasons that way—project carrot, deadline stick, praise carrot, shame stick—until the road itself disappeared and only the prodding remained.

Joshua's string cross from last night sat in his mind's eye, still humble on the table.

Carrot. Stick. Two poles. A fork for livestock.

Plus sign. Two strokes that join.

The bus arrived in a sigh of brakes. Michael tagged on and found a seat by the window. Rain-smear turned the street into an impressionist painting of wheelie bins.

He texted Joshua: Path: carrot/stick → + → cross?

Joshua, who kept hours that suggested either holiness or unemployment (he had a job; the holiness was optional), replied within a minute: Go on. Use your words. Bus brains are allowed to be drafty.

Michael typed with one thumb:

Carrot and stick are either/or engines. Move toward / move away. Useful for training dogs and bad cultures.
Plus sign is the same geometry as a cross without the history: join, add, relate.
Cross is plus sign that went through Rome and came back carrying weight.
So maybe incentives are the baby version of a deeper structure: not just push-pull, but fastening.

Joshua: If you laugh on the bus I'll know you've seen it.

Michael did laugh—once, sharp, the kind that makes the person opposite glance up and then politely invent a reason to look at their phone. Because it was funny, in the cosmic sense: the whole moral technology of reward and punishment strutting about as if it owned the road, while underneath it a quieter shape kept saying and. Not or. And.

Addition, not mere herding.

He watched a parent further down the aisle negotiate a toddler's meltdown with a biscuit (carrot) and a firm no (stick) and then, when both failed, simply hold the child until the weather passed (something else—presence, fastening, the horizontal beam in miniature).

The bus took a corner. Michael's bag leaned into his leg. Everything continued to fit, annoyingly.

At his stop he got off into air that smelled of wet stone. The office building accepted him with its usual glass indifference. In the lift he saw his own face reflected: a man who had begun the week trying not to exist hard enough to unplug experience, and was now giggling about geometry on public transport. Progress, of a sort.

Lunchtime he met Joshua in the little park between the office blocks—two benches, one optimistic tree, a flock of pigeons running a protection racket for crumbs.

'Carrot and stick,' Joshua said, stealing a crisp from Michael's packet, 'keep you moving. They don't tell you what the moving is for. Plus says: these belong together. Cross says: belonging costs, and the cost was not theoretical.'

'I don't want to baptise every corporate plus icon,' Michael said. 'I've sat through too many slide decks where a plus sign meant synergy and synergy meant redundancies with better fonts.'

'Then don't,' Joshua said. 'Keep the distinction. Baby geometry. Grown history. The joke is only load-bearing if you refuse to let marketing steal it.'

Michael leaned back on the bench. A cyclist went past with a small flag of mud. 'The laugh on the bus was relief. I've been trying to climb out of push-pull ethics—be good or get hit, be good or miss the treat—and the week keeps handing me joins instead. Experience and thought. Sacred and ordinary. Vertical and horizontal. Even the old data file: field and field and field.'

'Both/And franchise expanding,' Joshua said. 'Next you'll be putting plus signs on the beer mats.'

'Only if we can ban the word synergy.'

'Banned. Out of stock with superiority.'

They sat until the hour threatened employment consequences, then stood. Joshua punched Michael's shoulder lightly, a horizontal sacrament.

'Dishes tonight,' Joshua said. 'My place. Don't be clever about the cross until you've held a plate. Clever is cheap. Soap is true.'

Michael walked back to the glass building with the plus sign from the bus shelter still glowing in memory—crude marker, kind instruction, baby geometry on a wet wall.

At his desk he opened a notebook tab—digital, this time—and wrote three lines he did not show anyone:

Carrot/stick = push-pull.
Plus = join.
Cross = join that remembers the cost.

Then he answered three emails without using the word synergy, which felt like a tiny moral victory of the kind that does not get medals and does not need them.

Somewhere under the carrots and sticks of the working day, a quieter arithmetic continued: not merely toward and away, but with.

Chapter 11

Shame Into Love

960 words

Joshua's kitchen on Thursday night was smaller than Michael's and more honest about it. Magnets held takeaway menus, a postcard from somewhere with too much sky, and the Both/And beer mat, already soft at one corner from fridge condensation.

'Apron,' Joshua said, throwing him a tea towel. 'You wash. I dry. No metaphysics until the cutlery sings.'

Michael washed. Hot water, cheap lemon soap, the domestic squeak of plates becoming innocent again. It was hard to be grand with foam on your wrists. That was the point. He scrubbed a baking tray that had known sausages and, briefly, fire, until the black bits surrendered. Joshua dried with the efficient flick of someone who has lived alone long enough to respect a clean rack.

They worked through the stack: pans that had known tomatoes, glasses with fingerprint histories, a single stubborn mug that had seen things. The radio muttered a phone-in about traffic. A caller was furious about temporary lights on the ring road; the presenter made sympathetic noises that cost nothing. Rain ticked the window in a rhythm too irregular to be symbolic and too steady to ignore.

Michael almost started a sentence about Rome and stopped himself, hands still in the water. Joshua noticed and said nothing, which was its own kind of applause.

When the last fork was away, Joshua poured tea without asking and sat on the counter like a teenager who had never left and a man who had come back on purpose.

'Last night you wrote do not let this stay merely clever,' he said. 'Good line. Now keep it.'

Michael leaned against the sink. 'I keep almost turning the cross into a party trick. Look, geometry! Look, history! Watch me join Rome to a plus sign and a bus poster. It plays well. It would get nods in a pub. And every time I do it in my head I feel the shame bit slide off—the bit that makes the symbol expensive—and what's left is a diagram wearing a costume.'

Joshua nodded. 'Shame into love is not a rebrand exercise. You don't take the humiliation off the wood with a clever wipe. The claim—if you let the claim near you—is that love went into the shame and didn't treat it as a stain to be managed by PR. That's harder. It doesn't fit on a mat.'

'Can it fit in a kitchen?'

'If it can't, burn the theology books and keep the soap,' Joshua said. 'I'm serious. If your big shapes don't survive ordinary repair work—dishes, apologies, showing up when you'd rather be impressive—then they're hobbies. Hobbies are fine. Don't call them the axis of the world.'

Michael looked at his hands. Clean now. Still the same hands that had typed, held, mistaken. 'I don't want a hobby. I also don't want a club. Clubs are either/or with membership fees.'

'Then take the middle path that isn't mush,' Joshua said. 'Let the structure teach you how to stay. Vertical: something about the whole that isn't just your mood. Horizontal: the person in front of you, including me when I'm annoying, including you when you're tired and not done. Join: the cost of not dropping either beam when holding both hurts.'

The fridge clicked. The beer mat gleamed faintly under the kitchen bulb.

Michael said, quieter, 'When I was younger I thought love was a feeling you waited to be ambushed by. Then I thought it was a moral scoreboard. Now I wonder if it's more like… refusing to let shame have the last word without pretending shame was never in the room.'

Joshua's eyes were bright. Not tears, exactly. Attention. 'There. That's not clever. That's adult. Keep speaking like that and the diagrams can come back as servants instead of masters.'

They took the tea to the small sofa. A film neither of them cared about played with the sound low, a moving wallpaper of car chases. Michael found he could sit without pacing. The week's bricks were still stacked in him—experience, no pure nothing, one room, no edge, museum, both/and, delimiters, crossbeams, plus signs—but for once he was not trying to build a cathedral before bedtime.

'Thank you for not letting me get away with smart,' he said.

'Someone has to,' Joshua said. 'The world is full of smart that never did the washing-up. It writes books and abandons people. I'm not interested in you becoming that. You're allowed to be brilliant. You're not allowed to be brilliant instead.'

Michael laughed into his mug. 'You'd make a terrible cult leader.'

'I'd make an excellent one,' Joshua said. 'That's why I wash dishes. Countermeasure.'

Later, walking home under streetlights that made the wet road look varnished, Michael felt the difference in his body: less spark, more weight. The good kind. The kind that means a thought has agreed to live downstairs with the laundry and the bills and the people. A fox crossed the road ahead of him with the unhurried confidence of a creature that has never needed a theory of the whole, and Michael found himself wishing it well, which was ridiculous and also fine.

He texted Joshua from the hall of his own flat: Cross not allowed to stay clever. Logged.

Joshua: Good. Sleep. Tomorrow the world gets wider than our two beams. Be careful and kind.

Michael set the phone face down. He hung his coat. He filled a glass of water and left it by the bed like a small treaty with morning.

In the dark before sleep he did not diagram anything. He only noticed that shame and love were both still in the room with him—not resolved, not cancelled—

—fastened, for now, at a quiet join he did not yet have a better word for than stay.

Chapter 12

Moon, Star, And Rules About Pictures

938 words

Friday morning Michael nearly walked past the display without seeing it, which is how most of the important things in cities survive.

A bookshop window: new paperbacks, a novel with a lighthouse on the cover, a cookbook promising joy in thirty minutes, and—tucked at the edge—a small stand of comparative religion titles. One spine showed a crescent and star in clean gold foil. Another showed a cross. Another a wheel. The window dresser had arranged them like polite guests at a dinner where nobody wanted a fight.

Michael stopped. The glass threw his face back at him between the books, a ghost browser.

He knew just enough to know he did not know enough. That was not modesty as fashion. That was factual inventory. Islam was not a flavour of the week he had lived inside. It was a vast lived world with prayers and law and poetry and ordinary people buying milk—millions of kitchens, millions of arguments, millions of kindnesses that had never asked for his opinion.

Joshua's text from the night before returned: Tomorrow the world gets wider than our two beams. Be careful and kind.

He went into the shop because standing on the pavement staring felt like the start of a different mistake. A bell above the door made a polite sound. Somewhere deeper in the shop a child was being negotiated away from a display of stickers. The comparative shelf smelled of paper and dust and the faint vanilla of glue. He read three back covers and put two books back because wanting to own a subject is not the same as being ready to host it. He bought one slender book of essays by people who actually knew what they were talking about, and he stood at the counter with the quiet embarrassment of a man purchasing humility in softcover form.

The bookseller did not comment. Bookseller grace is one of the underrated civilisations.

On the way to the park he passed a bus stop advert for a streaming series about ancient empires and felt the old itch to connect everything to everything. He let the itch pass without scratching. That, too, was practice.

At lunch he met Joshua in the park again. The optimistic tree had gained a plastic bag in its branches, flapping like a surrender flag.

'I almost did a diagram,' Michael said. 'Moon and star next to the cross next to the plus sign. Neat little row. Then I heard myself becoming a man who collects other people's sacred things like fridge magnets.'

Joshua unwrapped a sandwich. 'Good catch. Rules about pictures matter. Not because God is frightened of ink. Because people live under symbols, and if you drag those symbols into your private museum without love, you're not studying—you're shoplifting.'

'I don't want to shoplift,' Michael said. 'I also don't want to pretend the week has only been a Christian geometry lesson with a philosophical porch. The high street has more than one kind of certainty. The museum claim—everything fits—can't mean everything gets translated into my beams by force.'

'Both/And with teeth,' Joshua said. 'Unity without erasure. You can notice a crescent the way you notice a glove on a wall: it is here, it belongs to the field of what-is. You cannot decide it secretly means your story in a clever font. That's just either/or wearing a smile.'

Michael turned the book in his hands. 'There's a discipline in not drawing certain things. I used to think that was only fear. Sometimes it is fear. Sometimes it's respect with a backbone—refusing the cheap picture that makes strangers easier to own.'

A woman walked past with a pushchair; a man on a bench fed pigeons with the fatal generosity that creates avian empires. The park continued being many lives long.

'So what's the move?' Joshua asked. 'For today. Not for the whole library of civilisation.'

'Careful humility,' Michael said, tasting the phrase. 'Read before I speak. Ask before I map. Leave empty fields in my own data where other people's names go—not Unknown as a dismissal, but reserved seats I don't fill with my own face.'

Joshua raised his coffee in a small salute. 'That'll do. That's horizontal beam work across difference. Vertical remains the whole that holds all of us without being any of our mascots alone.'

Michael thought of the preacher and the sceptic, of how quickly certainty became a weapon, of how his own week could become a weapon if he got drunk on pattern-finding. Pattern-finding was a joy. Joy without reverence curdled.

'I will not,' he said slowly, 'use anyone's moon and star as a prop in my clever week. If they appear later, they appear as guests—with rules, with names, with the right to correct me.'

'Write that down,' Joshua said. 'Next to do not let the cross stay merely clever. Same family of vow.'

Michael did, on a receipt from the bookshop, because the notebook was at home and vows should not always wait for ideal stationery:

Other people's sacred signs are not my alphabet to rearrange for fun.
Curiosity is allowed. Capture is not.
Empty field ≠ blank me; empty field = seat for a voice I haven't earned yet.

He walked back to work with the book under his arm and the plastic bag still flapping in the tree. The city did not applaud. Cities rarely do when you choose not to be an idiot.

That felt, in its quiet way, like progress: not a new symbol conquered—

—but a picture not taken, on purpose, out of something that might one day be called love if he kept practising.

Chapter 13

Who Is Like God?

818 words

Friday evening the flat was full of the smell of toast and the particular silence that follows a week of thinking too loudly.

Michael had the slender book open on the table and was not reading it. He was stuck on his own name, which is a strange place to be stuck, like tripping over the doormat of yourself.

Joshua arrived with a bottle of something soft and fizzy because the week did not need wine's opinions. He took one look at Michael's face and said, 'You've found etymology. I know that face. It's the face of a man who has googled himself in Hebrew and is now having feelings.'

'Michael,' Michael said. 'Who is like God? That's the question form. A name that is not an answer. A name that is a shove away from the throne.'

Joshua sat. 'And?'

'And parents hand it out like a jumper. Happy Christmas, son: rhetorical monotheism. No pressure.'

Joshua grinned. 'Better than being named after a car.'

'I'm serious. All week I've been building—careful bricks, vows about cleverness, empty fields for other people's symbols—and then I remember my own label is a warning label. Who is like God? Implied answer: not you, mate. Not the archangel either, not really—you're named after the question the archangel's name is. You're a souvenir mug from a cosmic argument.'

Joshua poured the fizz. Bubbles rose with industrious joy. 'You're not the archangel. Good. Archangels have terrible hours and very little downtime. You're a man who washes up and laughs on buses. The name isn't a job offer. It's a posture.'

'Posture,' Michael repeated.

'Chin not quite so high. Knees able to bend. The question keeps a gap open where idolatry wants to weld you shut. Who is like God? If you answer me with a straight face, you've failed the exam. If you answer no one and then go about your business with a bit of awe left in the tank, you've understood the nickname.'

Michael rubbed his face. 'It sits oddly next to the week. Experience that won't unplug—almost feels like a throne room. One room, no outside—claustrophobic grandeur. Everything fits—totalising. Then the crossbeams—easy to cast yourself as the clever joiner of heaven and earth. The name is a bucket of water.'

'Good,' Joshua said. 'You needed a bucket. I was going to throw one eventually.'

They drank. The toast cooled into the edible form of regret; Michael made more. Ordinary salvage.

'When we were kids,' Joshua said, 'you used to introduce yourself like the name was a dare. Michael. Full. As if daring the room to see an angel and finding a boy with scraped knees. I always liked that. You weren't pretending to be celestial. You were stubborn about being present.'

'Present is the axiom,' Michael said softly. 'Start here. Not start on a cloud.'

'Exactly. Who-is-like-God is not you-are-nothing. It's you-are-not-the-whole, which is what makes room for love. If you were the whole, there'd be no horizontal beam—no neighbour, no brother, no stranger with a crescent on a book spine you refuse to steal. The question protects relation.'

Michael wrote in the notebook, hand slower than earlier in the week:

Michael = who is like God?
Not a promotion. A permanent open question.
I am not the archangel. I am not the whole.
The gap is where other people fit—and where worship doesn't collapse into mirror-work.

Joshua read it. 'Frame that next to the dish vow.'

'I'm going to need a bigger wall.'

'You're going to need a life,' Joshua said. 'Walls are for hanging tools. The work is elsewhere.'

They moved to easier talk for a while: a funny email from a client, a film Joshua half-recommended, the neighbour's door that always slammed as if making a point about ontology. The evening softened. Michael felt the name settle from alarm back into clothing—something he wore, not something that wore him.

At the door, as Joshua left, Michael said, 'If I start acting like the answer to my own name, you'll tell me.'

'I'll do worse,' Joshua said. 'I'll make you dry every plate in the house while I recite the question in different accents.'

'That's abuse.'

'That's love with props.'

After the door closed, Michael stood in the hall—the same hall that had refused to lead outside of here—and said his name once, quietly, like a man checking a tool.

'Michael.'

Not a throne.
Not a joke.
A question that kept the ceiling from coming down and crushing the room where brothers drank fizz and ate second toast.

He slept without diagrams.

In the morning there would be a text, and a footnote about rakes, and the end of something that was only the first act—

—but Friday night belonged to the gap in the name, and the mercy of not being God, and the strange relief of answering the ancient question with a life instead of a speech.

Chapter 14

Gabriel Delivers

768 words

Saturday morning arrived without a brass section.

Michael woke to grey light and the awareness of his own name still sitting on the bedside table of his mind like a glass of water from the night before. He made tea. He did not argue with chairs. Progress.

His phone buzzed while the kettle worked.

Unknown number. Then, as the message preview slid up, not unknown after all—an old friend-of-a-friend thread, a group chat that had been dormant so long it had practically fossilised, the kind of digital drawer where people go to become Christmas round-robins.

The message was from Gabriel.

Not an archangel. A human Gabriel: works in logistics, laughs with his whole face, once helped Michael move a sofa up a stairwell that violated several laws of physics and good sense. They had not spoken in months. Life had done that thing life does where it piles boxes in the doorway of intentions.

The text said:

Keep going.

That was all.

No emoji. No explanation. No hope you're well throat-clearing. Just two words, delivered with the blunt grace of a parcel left on the correct step.

Michael sat down hard enough that the Chair of Being—formerly Non-Being, reformed by buttocks and time—creaked a welcome.

He typed: ??
Deleted it.
Typed: You too.
Deleted that too, because it sounded like a poster in a gym.

He rang Joshua.

'Gabriel texted me Keep going,' Michael said. 'Out of nowhere. From a dead group chat. As if he'd been reading the minutes of my week.'

Joshua was quiet long enough for Michael to hear toast being chewed. 'Had you told him any of it?'

'No. We haven't spoken since—what—spring? Sofa era feels like another geological period.'

'Then you have two honest options,' Joshua said. 'One: coincidence, brain pattern-matching, the museum accepting a random exhibit and you hanging a plaque on it. Two: the field is friendlier than your sarcasm. Either way, the instruction is good. Keep going is not become a prophet. It's don't put the tools down just because the first wall looks straight.'

Michael stared at the two words until they blurred. 'Gabriel. Messenger name. I'm trying not to get drunk on that.'

'Don't,' Joshua said firmly. 'You just spent last night learning your own name is a question that blocks thrones. Don't build a throne for a text message. Receive it like a sandwich from a friend. Nourishing. Not a coronation.'

'How did he even—'

'Maybe he dreamed you looked tired. Maybe someone mentioned you. Maybe his thumb slipped on an old intention. You can ask him later without turning it into a relic. For now, answer like a person.'

Michael hung up, breathed, and wrote back:

Thanks. Needed that more than is dignified to admit. How are you?

Three minutes later: Tired. Moving house again. Saw your name in the chat list and thought of the sofa and your stubborn face. Keep going means keep going. Not only you. Me too.

Michael laughed—soft, relieved, human. The cosmos could keep its brass section. This was better: two men in different postcodes, both tired, both still in the only room there is, passing a small coal of encouragement so it wouldn't go out.

He sent: Sofa PTSD never left. If you need a horizontal beam that lifts, call me.

Gabriel: I might. Go eat breakfast.

Michael ate breakfast. Eggs. Toast that did not burn. Tea that did not go cold while he argued with absence, because for once he was not arguing with absence—he was chewing.

He opened the notebook and wrote, under the name-lines:

Gabriel delivers (human department).
Message: Keep going.
Meaning: not a throne. A sandwich. Shared.
Answer: thank you, and same to you, and the sofa stands ready.

Mid-morning he walked to the corner shop for milk he actually needed. The high street was Saturday-busy: prams, football shirts, a charity collector with a smile of professional hope. No amplifier preacher. No loud sceptic. Just the museum in casual clothes.

His phone buzzed once more—Joshua this time: Act carefully with signs. Act generously with friends. Hellfire Club research this afternoon? Purely historical. I bring cake. You bring scepticism.

Michael: Deal. Keep going applies to cake.

Joshua: Especially to cake.

He walked home with the milk and the odd buoyancy of a man who has been told, by someone not trying to convert him, that the week is allowed to continue. Not because the bricks were finished. Because unfinished is not the same as failed.

Keep going.

Two words. Load-bearing. Horizontal. Delivered without wings—

—which was, Michael thought, rather the style of the whole business so far.

Chapter 15

The Hellfire Club (Historical Footnote)

936 words

Saturday afternoon Joshua arrived with cake in a slightly crushed box and a laptop already warm from research.

'Hellfire Club,' he announced, as if naming a pub. 'Historical footnote. Rakes, robes, caves if you believe the tourist board, pies allegedly named after sacred things by men who thought blasphemy was a personality. Half of it is legend layered on gossip layered on someone trying to sell a book in 1790. The other half is still embarrassing enough to count.'

Michael cleared the table. The string cross was gone; in its place, crumbs and a shared plate. 'Why are we doing this at the end of the week? As a cautionary tale?'

'As a mirror with warts,' Joshua said. 'You've spent days building something that could, if you got lonely and vain, tip into cosplay. Secret knowledge. Special club. Mock the sacred so you feel tall. Or the opposite error: polish the sacred until it can't survive a joke. Hellfire is what happens when satire forgets to love the thing it cuts, and seriousness forgets to breathe.'

They read in tandem—proper sources where possible, sceptical notes where the stories smelled of brandy. Eighteenth-century gentlemen playing at damnation between courses. Empire money. Bored privilege inventing a nightlife of inverted pieties. One account mentioned secret names; another denied the club had ever been more than a dinner with costumes; a third insisted on caves and thunder. The details slid about depending on the author; the shape held: mockery as membership badge.

Michael found a reproduction of a period caricature and winced. Men in robes, faces arranged into daring. A table of excess. The artist had not been kind, and still the subjects seemed to be enjoying the unkindness as publicity. 'They're not funny,' he said. 'Not now. Maybe not then. It's all elbowing for status. Look how brave we are, insulting what other people kneel to, while the servants bring more wine.'

'Satire needs love,' Joshua said, cutting cake. 'Or it becomes the sneer it mocks. Love doesn't mean soft focus. Love means you still want the good of what you're criticising—or the good of the people tangled in it. Hellfire wanted a thrill and a club. That's not critique. That's costume.'

Michael ate cake and thought of the high-street preacher and the loud sceptic, of his own temptation to be clever about crosses and moons and names. 'I've felt the pull,' he admitted. 'Not robes. The modern kind. Private language. We who see the joins. Beer mats as initiation. It's funny until it isn't—until someone outside the joke becomes furniture.'

Joshua pushed the laptop aside. 'So we close Act I with a rule, not a robe. If we gather—brothers, friends, later whoever the story brings—we gather to listen and repair, not to cosplay damnation or enlightenment. No pies named after holy things for the giggle. No secret alphabet that makes outsiders unclean. The Both/And shop stays open late and still doesn't sell superiority.'

Michael fetched the notebook. The week's pages had filled with a surprising steadiness: axiom, nothing's failure, one room, no edge, museum, blind guides, beer-mat shop, delimiters, crossbeams, plus signs, dishes, careful pictures, a name that was a question, a text that said keep going.

He wrote:

Hellfire footnote: mockery without love is just empire in a mask.
Satire's job is surgery, not trophy-hunting.
Club policy: no damnation cosplay. No enlightenment cosplay.
If it isn't kind under the joke, bin the joke.

Joshua read, nodded, then drew a small square on a fresh beer mat he had produced from his pocket like a magician with modest powers.

ACT I — CLOSED
Tools kept. Thrones refused.
Cake mandatory.

Michael laughed. 'That's not how books end chapters.'

'It's how we end weeks,' Joshua said. 'Books can catch up.'

They sat until the light thickened toward evening. Outside, Saturday did Saturday things: football scores, shopping bags, a distant siren that belonged to someone else's emergency and still, annoyingly, fitted in the same field. Inside, two brothers finished cake and did not found a religion.

'Next week the brains will try to arrive late and take credit,' Joshua said, half teasing, half preview. 'Allegedly. Prediction errors for breakfast. You can be ready without being armoured.'

'Keep going,' Michael said, and the words tasted different now—less like a thunderclap, more like a packed lunch.

'Keep going,' Joshua agreed. 'And when you want to be Hellfire-clever, wash something. Horizontal beam. Works on pride like lemon on grease.'

At the door they hugged—brief, British, sufficient. Michael stood alone in the hall afterward and looked at the doors he had opened on Monday. Still internal. Still here. The map still had no edge made of pure nothing. The museum was still open. The shop still refused false dilemmas. The cross still refused to be merely clever. The name still asked its question. The messenger had been a tired man moving house.

He put the Act I beer mat on his own fridge, opposite the electricity bill.

Sacred and ordinary.

Then he washed the cake plates, dried them, put them away, and turned off the kitchen light with the quiet satisfaction of a man who has finished a true thing without needing a robe for the afterparty.

Somewhere—everywhere—the week completed itself the only way weeks can: by becoming the next morning, still inside the only room, still offering the same unkillable is.

Act I closed with a soft snap.

The tools stayed on the table for Act II.

And the last crumb of cake, which had no theology at all, was eaten without residue of shame—

—which was, as endings go, a surprisingly good footnote.

Chapter 16

The Brain That Arrived Late (Allegedly)

944 words

Monday of week two began with a podcast and a lie so polite it almost sounded like science.

The kettle had already done its honest work. Steam softened the kitchen window. Somewhere a neighbour slammed a car door with the confidence of a person who did not currently require a theory of mind. Michael envied that confidence for three seconds and then returned to the problem of being awake inside a skull that other people wanted to demote.

Michael was washing a mug when the voice in his earbuds explained, cheerfully, that consciousness was a late invention. Evolution had spent ages on kidneys and claws, the host said, and only near the end had the universe produced awareness—like pudding after a long dinner, or a software feature bolted on after the hardware shipped.

'Late dessert,' Michael muttered. 'Allegedly.'

The claim was happening as experience. That was the problem. You could not announce that the screen had arrived after the film while the announcement was the film.

Joshua rang while the neuroscientist was still mid-metaphor about neural fireworks.

'You're listening to something that will annoy you,' Joshua said. Not a question.

'Consciousness as afterthought. Brains first, mind as bonus content.'

Joshua made the small sound he reserved for goals scored from impossible angles. 'Brother's axiom. You don't explain the datum by something you only know through the datum. Not without smuggling.'

Michael dried the mug carefully, as if cleanliness might help. 'They mean well. Scans, timing, lesions. Brains are extraordinary. I don't want to insult the furniture.'

'Furniture is exquisite,' Joshua said. 'Rearrange every chair in the house if you like. You still don't get behind the room by measuring the sofa.'

Outside, a bin lorry performed its weekly sacrament of noise. Michael turned the podcast down and left it running, a background sermon about synapses.

'They can map every millimetre,' he said. 'Correlate the spark with the thought. Show me which patch lights up when I taste coffee. None of that puts the tasting outside the tasting. The map is drawn on the territory.'

'And the map is also experience,' Joshua said. 'You're not sneering at neurology. You're refusing a magic trick where the rabbit explains the hat by hopping later.'

Michael almost laughed. The week-one bricks were still warm under his feet: the cold floor that would not unplug; nothing that could not clock in; doors into more of the same room. Now the systems people were arriving with white coats and good intentions, offering to demote the whole show to an epilogue.

'What if the brain does generate it?' he asked, because honesty required the hard version.

'Then the generating still appears as this,' Joshua said. 'You never get a vantage where experience is optional and brains are primary. The "primary" claim is written in the same ink. Late dessert is a story told inside the meal.'

Michael set the mug down. On the counter, his phone showed a waveform of the podcast host's voice—peaks and troughs of confidence.

'Allegedly late,' he said. 'Fundamentally first. Depending on whether you're watching the clock or watching the watching.'
