NPC theory, done properly, was not a licence to despise the crowd. It was a map of why the crowd could not all burn as bright as the people who stayed up inventing new doors at one in the morning—and why those people still owed the crowd their patience.

He thought of last night’s dialetheism tabs, of Cameron asleep on principle, of his own mornings on autopilot—teeth, keys, the same route to the same desk. High agency was a weather system, not a permanent rank. Some hours he was the expensive subprocess. Some hours he was the man ordering the usual without invention. Both could be true without a moral ladder appearing under his feet.

Joshua stood to leave. “Kindness version means you still build hard things. You just stop needing contempt as fuel.”

“Contempt is cheap fuel,” Michael said. “Burns dirty.”

“There you go.”

He finished his coffee.

It was ordinary.

That was the point.

Chapter 32

High Agency, Low Bandwidth World

894 words

Evening. Home. The kind of domestic quiet that looks like peace from the street and feels like a negotiation from the sofa.

Michael had been talking—carefully at first, then with the heat that always arrived when the numbers got large. Distant deaths. Preventable misery. The way the news turned horror into weather if it happened far enough away. He could feel his own voice climbing the ladder of urgency while the room stayed stubbornly local: laundry basket, half-done homework on the table, the television offering a programme about houses someone else could afford.

She was tired. Not wicked. Not shallow. Tired in the way bodies get tired after a day that had already spent its care budget on the people in front of them.

“I can’t do this tonight,” she said. “I need to sleep.”

The irony landed in Michael’s chest like a coin he had flipped a thousand times. He had just said that millions die because people protect their comfort. She had just protected her sleep. The universe, which has a sense of humour with excellent timing, did not even bother to underline it.

He said, “Right,” because right is what high-agency people say when they are trying not to turn love into a tribunal.

She went upstairs. A door closed with the soft finality of bandwidth hitting zero.

Michael sat in the glow of the muted television and felt the old isolation rise—the sense of being a furnace in a street of carefully banked fires.

He texted Joshua, then deleted the text, then wrote a better one.

Michael: How do you live as a high-agency person who can’t unsee the scale, in a world where most people have low bandwidth and go to bed?

Joshua called instead of typing. His voice was road-noise and kindness.

“You don’t live by requiring them to match your temperature,” Joshua said. “You live by treating their bandwidth as physics, not betrayal.”

“It feels like betrayal.”

“I know. Feeling isn’t the same as a court case.” A pause. A car horn somewhere on Joshua’s end. “Proximity bias. Psychic numbing. Finite empathy. You’ve read the papers. Tonight you met the papers in a dressing gown.”

Michael walked to the kitchen and opened the fridge for no reason, which is what people do when ontology gets personal. “My comfort feels insignificant next to the horror.”

“Your comfort is insignificant as a moral goal,” Joshua said. “Your capacity is not insignificant as an instrument. If you burn the instrument to prove you care, the horror keeps the win.”

Michael shut the fridge. “That’s the speech people give when they want permission to stay small.”

“No,” Joshua said, patient. “That’s the speech people give when they want you still standing in five years. High agency is not infinite agency. Low bandwidth around you is not a personal insult. It’s the median climate. You build inside the climate. You don’t wait for the climate to become you.”

Cameron, who had a talent for appearing when metaphors needed grounding, texted the group:

Cameron: Also: wanting sleep after work is not ‘not caring about distant deaths’. It’s not being a villain for having a nervous system.

Michael stared at the phone, then typed:

Michael: I know. The ache is still real.

Cameron: Ache can be real without being a mandate to judge the household.

Joshua, still on the call, must have heard the notification tone. “He’s right again. Annoying habit.”

Michael almost laughed. “I’m not satisfied to ignore the problems.”

“Then don’t ignore them,” Joshua said. “Select. Leverage. Systems. The few people who can walk with you. Measure by what moves, not by how many bystanders get it. Emotional consensus from the tired majority is a terrible KPI.”

Michael looked up the stairs. Love lived up there, bandwidth-limited and real. Work lived in him, scale-haunted and real. The conflict was not a puzzle with one tidy piece missing. It was the ordinary tragedy of finite creatures who can model infinite need.

He said goodnight to Joshua. He washed a plate that did not need washing. He stood in the doorway of the bedroom long enough to hear steady breathing, then retreated like a man who had remembered that presence can be quiet.

In his notebook he wrote one sentence for the morning:

High agency, low bandwidth world: care without requiring a matching fire in every room.

Then, smaller:

Sleep is not the enemy. Resentment is.

He did not sleep quickly. High agency rarely does. But when sleep came, it came without a speech, which was progress of a sort the horror statistics would never applaud and the household needed anyway.

He almost reopened the conversation upstairs—one more paragraph about the millions, one more attempt to make the temperature match. He stopped with his hand on the banister. That would not have been courage. That would have been extracting agreement from a tired nervous system to soothe his own loneliness.

Outside, the town dimmed room by room.

Inside, Michael practised the hardest skill on the list: staying awake to the scale of things without turning the people he loved into evidence for the prosecution.

Tomorrow he would work. Tomorrow the scale would still be there. Tonight the work was simpler and somehow harder: let low bandwidth be real without calling it evil, and let his own fire burn without demanding the whole street catch.

Chapter 33

The Mask

803 words

Michael had a face for the world and a face for the work, and the seam between them was starting to itch.

It was not a villain’s mask. Villains get better production design. This was the ordinary social prosthesis of a man whose interior volume exceeded the room’s noise limit. In meetings he dialled down the eschatology. At dinners he left the distant dead in their statistics. With almost everyone he performed a milder Michael—interested, competent, not currently on fire—so that the people in his orbit would not run.

The double life worked.

It was also expensive.

He noticed the tax on a Thursday, of all days, while smiling at a joke he did not find funny and feeling the smile arrive three milliseconds late, like a special effect rendered on old hardware. Afterward, in the car, he sat with both hands on the wheel and did not start the engine.

Experiment log, informal:

Unmask attempt one: late-night seriousness with a friend. Result: subject changed topic to football, then left early.

Unmask attempt two: half-confession of vocation intensity to someone who loved him. Result: worry, then gentle requests to be “a bit less.”

Unmask attempt three: full voltage in a group chat that had once seemed safe. Result: silence long enough to teach him something about sample size.

He was not inventing the pain. Rejection has a clean empirical flavour. You can taste it even when you explain it.

Joshua met him at the edge of the Tyne, because brothers sometimes need water nearby when the conversation is about faces.

“I keep testing authenticity,” Michael said. “The evidence says I can’t. Nobody tolerates unmasked me.”

Joshua watched a gull argue with another gull about nothing. “Nobody in the sample you’ve drawn.”

“That’s a comfort line.”

“It’s also statistics,” Joshua said. “Ordinary orbits have near-zero tolerance for your intensity. That doesn’t prove the global claim. It proves you keep running the same experiment in the same lab and calling the repeated burn a law of nature.”

Michael kicked a stone. It skittered, unimpressed by metaphysics. “The mask is necessary.”

“In low-alignment rooms, yes,” Joshua said. “Necessary isn’t the same as total. You can shrink the surface area that requires performance. You can stop high-stakes unmasking where the prior is already zero. You can hunt better rooms instead of demanding the current ones grow new ears.”

A runner went past with the focused face of someone whose only cosmology was pace.

Michael said, “The pain of rejection every time I lower it—”

“Is a cost you should stop paying for free information,” Joshua finished. “You already know those people can’t hold it. Stop asking them to. That’s not cowardice. That’s not wasting the instrument.”

Cameron texted, because the universe enjoyed a chorus:

Cameron: Also stop calling it ‘the mask’ like you’re Batman. You’re just code-switching. Everyone does. Yours is just louder when you don’t.

Michael showed Joshua. Joshua snorted.

“He’s half right,” Joshua said. “Everyone softens. Your version is costlier because the gap is wider. Don’t romanticise the gap into a destiny of permanent exile. Don’t pretend the gap isn’t real either. Both.”

Michael looked at the water. The water did not care about his face. That was restful.

He thought of the café’s radio man, of tired partners, of recruiters and narrow paths waiting in other chapters of the week. The mask was not only social grease. It was how a high-agency creature avoided becoming a wrecking ball in rooms built for quieter weather.

He also thought—quietly, without the old self-pity—that a life spent entirely in costume would eventually forget which skin was under the fabric.

“Partial matches,” he said aloud. “Not full acceptance from everyone. Just enough shared seriousness that the tax drops.”

Joshua nodded. “Hunt those. Keep the rest kind and shallow on purpose. And when you do unmask, choose audiences like you’d choose a climbing partner. Not everyone belongs on the same rope.”

They walked back toward the car. Michael’s public face settled into place without drama—eyes softer, mouth ready for ordinary weather. Under it, the private fire did not go out. It simply stopped demanding a stage from every pavement.

At the door he paused.

“Josh.”

“Mm.”

“If I ever start believing the mask is the real me—”

“I’ll be rude,” Joshua said. “It’s one of my spiritual gifts.”

Michael drove home in light traffic, performing nothing for the dashboard, and for twenty minutes the seam did not itch.

He thought of the double life not as a noir plot but as overhead—like running two operating systems on one machine until the fans scream. The fix was not to smash the machine. The fix was fewer contexts that required the second OS, and one or two people who could log into the real one without panic.

That, too, was data.

Chapter 34

Recruiter At The Fork

843 words

The email arrived with the cheerful professionalism of a man who got paid to open doors other people would call destinies.

Subject line polite. Body clean. Six-figure CTO. PE-backed. Near enough to home that the geography would not even punish him. AI, SaaS, automation, executive team, direct reports, growth phase—the whole modern hymn sheet. A recruiter named Tom, who had done his homework well enough to make flattery feel like research.

Michael read it once at the kitchen table with coffee.

He read it again standing up, as if posture might change the moral physics.

Outside, a bin lorry performed its sacramental racket. Inside, the early-stage thing he was building—local, awkward, still smelling of paint and promise—sat in his mind like a plant that had only just put down roots and would not survive being forgotten for a season of someone else’s exit timeline.

Easy path. Hard path.

He hated how cartoon the fork looked when he named it. Life was supposed to be more sophisticated than a morality play. And yet here was the play, emailed at 09:12, with a calendar link.

Joshua came round later with pastries that were an apology for being late and also just pastries.

“Read this,” Michael said.

Joshua read. Whistled once, soft. “That’s a real door.”

“It’s a broad path with excellent lighting,” Michael said. “I could walk through it and call it provision.”

“Could,” Joshua agreed. “Would it still be your life?”

Michael sat. The chair creaked in the vernacular of cheap furniture everywhere. “If I take the easy route I won’t half-take it. I’ll go all in. Comfort. Status. Enough money to forget what I was trying to build. That’s the trap of me—all or nothing.”

Joshua tore a pastry and did not offer empty comfort. “All-or-nothing is an engine. It’s also a cliff with nice branding.”

“I’m not looking for prep,” Michael said. “I’m looking at the fork. Live for the work that costs me, or live for the life that pays me to stop meaning it.”

His phone lit. Cameron, who had been looped in because friends are sometimes the quality-assurance department of the soul:

Cameron: Confidential chat with a recruiter is information, not marriage.

Michael: I know. Still a fork.

Cameron: Forks can be walked past without declaring one road dead forever. You’re dramatic when hungry.

Michael almost typed something sharp, then ate a pastry instead. Cameron was annoying in the specific way that keeps people alive.

Joshua said, “The narrow path only stays narrow if you keep choosing it on purpose. Not if you fuse your entire self to unbroken forward motion so hard that any pause feels like death.”

“If I stop I don’t know who I am,” Michael said, and heard how true and how dangerous the sentence was.

“You’re the one who chooses,” Joshua said. “The values were in you before this vehicle had a name. They’ll be in you if the shape changes. Don’t make annihilation the price of rest, and don’t make the PE job the only alternative to holiness. Those are not the only two towns on the map.”

Michael looked at the email signature again. Tom looked like a nice enough bloke. The job looked like a life many good people would take without shame. That was what made it hard. Evil would have been easier to refuse.

He thought of cleaners paid properly, of new parents, of ward-by-ward stubbornness, of the loneliness of waiting for co-pilgrims who wanted the journey and not only the rewards that appeared around high-agency weather. He thought of family upstairs and downstairs and the robot life of earning comfort while the inner vocation starved—and how that robot life felt, to him, worse than difficulty, worse even than the fantasy of vanishing into a forest with a laptop and quiet.

“I’m staying on the hard path,” he said at last. Not as theatre. As a decision that still had to be remade tomorrow.

Joshua nodded. “Then answer Tom like a free man, not a martyr. Polite. Clear. No drama. Keep your eyes open about runway and sleep. Martyrs who don’t sleep become problems for the people they meant to serve.”

Michael drafted the reply at the table, deleted the poetry, left the gratitude, closed the door without slamming it. Send.

The fork did not vanish. Forks rarely do. They just stop pretending to be the whole landscape.

He washed the coffee mugs. Joshua stole the last pastry with fraternal immunity.

Outside, the bin lorry moved on to the next street, efficient as empire, ordinary as Tuesday.

Michael checked the sent folder once, not for drama, for the adult pleasure of a decision that had used language carefully. No martyr essay. No contempt for Tom. No secret clause that the hard path meant self-erasure. Just: thank you, not now, building something local.

Inside, he felt the particular lightness of a costly choice made while still able to laugh—which is, if you are keeping score at home, one of the better signs you have not yet mistaken intensity for wisdom.

Chapter 35

Alpha Is Mum And Dad

827 words

Saturday morning, Michael fell down a research hole that began with a throwaway phrase and ended with wolves.

Someone online had used alpha the way people always used it—chin out, hierarchy porn, the fantasy of a ladder you climb by biting. Michael, who had been thinking about first and last things more than was strictly professional, typed a question he should have typed years ago:

Do wild wolves actually work like that?

The answer, when the better sources arrived, was the sort of correction that makes popular culture look like it had been drinking.

The rigid alpha–omega ladder had been built largely out of mid-century studies of captive wolves—unrelated animals forced together, stressed, inventing politics the way prisoners invent currencies. A famous book had helped the myth travel. Leadership seminars had rented the myth a suit. Dog trainers had turned it into a religion of dominance. Meanwhile, in the actual woods, packs were mostly families.

Mum. Dad. Kids.

The breeding pair were not tyrants who had fought their way up a corporate structure made of teeth. They were parents. Youngsters dispersed when they grew. The word alpha, applied like a badge of conquest, was a mistranslation of kinship into empire.

Michael sat back from the screen and laughed once, short and delighted.

Of course. Of course the universe’s favourite joke would be that the word people used for domination was, in better light, a word for looking after the den.

Joshua came in with two mugs and the expression of a man who had heard laughter and wanted to know if it was safe.

“Wolves,” Michael said. “Alpha isn’t the boss fight. Alpha is mum and dad.”

Joshua handed him a mug. “Say more.”

Michael turned the laptop. A careful paragraph about L. David Mech, about later corrections, about retiring the language for wild animals. Joshua read, eyebrows climbing.

“So the internet’s favourite masculinity cosplay,” Joshua said, “is a captivity artefact.”

“Mostly. Some researchers still say alpha pair casually. Still not the ladder myth.” Michael sipped. “I love it. The symbol people use for first and last, top and bottom, turns out to be family science wearing a Halloween costume called hierarchy.”

Joshua smiled. “Both/and shop opens again.”

“The myth is culturally real,” Michael said, ticking fingers. “People behave as if the ladder is true. The science says the ladder is largely wrong for the animals they stole it from. And then the same letters show up in other rooms—beginnings and endings, titles, claims about the whole.” He stopped himself before the sentence ran into later chapters. Timing was a kind of ethics.

Cameron texted into the void that friends maintain between each other:

Cameron: If this becomes a TED talk about how you’re the dad-wolf of Tyneside I’m leaving the chat.

Michael: It’s about bad science becoming folklore. And family being the real pack.

Cameron: Fine. Still not calling you alpha.

Joshua read over his shoulder. “Healthy boundary.”

They took the dogs out—metaphorical in Michael’s case, literal in the neighbourhood’s. On the path by the field a man barked orders at a bewildered spaniel in the name of dominance theory. The spaniel, who had never read a paper, elected to sniff a bin with the serene authority of someone who knew what actually mattered.

“There,” Michael said quietly. “Captive myth, live demo.”

Joshua watched the spaniel win without fighting. “Mum and dad would have just fed it and set a boundary. Less theatre. More pack.”

Michael thought of his own household—the negotiations, the tired evenings, the fierce protectiveness that did not need a crown. He thought of the hard path and the easy email and the way leadership language always tried to recruit animal blood for human ego.

Alpha, cleaned of the cosplay, was not a permission slip to dominate.

It was a reminder that the ones at the front of a healthy group are often the ones carrying food, teaching, staying when staying costs.

He said as much. Joshua bumped his shoulder, brother shorthand for landed.

On the walk home Michael felt the word differently in his mouth. Less armour. More apron. The culture would keep selling the ladder; ladders sell. But he had seen the family underneath, and once you see the den, the throne looks like furniture someone dragged into the wrong forest.

He opened his notes app at the gate and wrote:

Alpha is mum and dad.

The ladder was a stressed cage telling stories about itself.

Joshua, watching him type, said, “You’re going to connect this to the big letters eventually.”

“Eventually,” Michael said. “Not before the noticing is clean. Today is only the den.”

“Good. First things first is also a kind of alpha.”

Michael groaned. Joshua looked pleased with himself, which is a brother’s right under common law.

Then Michael put the phone away, because the next useful act was not symbolism.

It was making lunch—sandwiches, the unglamorous sacrament of packs that intend to stay together past the seminar.

Chapter 36

Omega Isn’t A Job

777 words

Once you pull on a thread marked alpha, the word omega comes away in your hand like a second sock.

Michael spent the afternoon with the less glamorous half of the myth.

In the popular story, omega was a job description. Lowest rung. Scapegoat. Stress-sponge. Sometimes jester, sometimes punchbag, always fixed—as if a living creature had been hired into a permanent role in someone else’s hierarchy and given no exit interview. Leadership blogs loved it. Fiction loved it. People who had been hurt loved having a word for the bottom of the pile, which was understandable and still not the same as zoology.

The better wolf science was dryer and kinder.

A fixed omega role looked a lot like something that appears when you jam unrelated animals into a space too small for their nerves. Captivity manufactures politics. Stress invents jobs. The scapegoat is often an artefact of a bad system, not a natural vocation written into fur.

Michael paced the living room with a pen behind his ear like a man late for an exam he had set himself.

“So omega isn’t a career,” he told the empty chair that sometimes stood in for Joshua and sometimes for God. “It’s what a broken arrangement does to whoever can’t leave.”

The empty chair, being furniture, did not argue.

He texted Joshua anyway.

Michael: Omega as fixed scapegoat = captivity artefact. Not a sacred job title.

Joshua: Then stop volunteering for it.

Michael stared at the reply. Brothers have a way of translating cosmology into homework.

He typed, deleted, typed:

Michael: I don’t volunteer—

Joshua: You take the hit so the room stays calm. You carry the intensity alone. You call it the hard path. Sometimes that’s vocation. Sometimes that’s you accepting a role a stressed pack invented because no one redesigned the cage.

The message sat there, glowing with the particular light of sentences that are true enough to sting.

Cameron, inevitably:

Cameron: If omega means ‘person who absorbs everyone else’s chaos’, that’s not mysticism, that’s a boundary problem.

Michael: I’m writing about wolves.

Cameron: You’re always writing about wolves. Set a calendar alert for ‘not my job’.

Michael laughed despite himself and put the phone face down.

He made tea properly this time—mug, bag, patience—and stood at the counter reconstructing the both/and without forcing a sermon.

The cultural omega was real as story: people got cast as lowest, treated as lowest, believed they were lowest. Stories shape behaviour. That mattered.

The scientific omega, for wild families, was mostly not a permanent identity. Young disperse. Roles flex. The pack is kinship more than corporate chart.

And somewhere beyond both, the old sacred sense of last things—endings, completions, the final letter in someone else’s alphabet—waited without needing him to pin it to a victim.

He did not need to decode every Greek ghost today. He only needed one clean thought:

Omega isn’t a job you owe the universe.

Scapegoat is what systems do when they refuse to grow up.

Joshua rang. “You angry at me?”

“No,” Michael said. “Annoyed at accuracy.”

“Good. Listen—there’s a difference between serving and being the designated shock absorber. One is love. The other is a captivity script with better PR. You can walk the hard path without applying for omega on LinkedIn.”

Michael looked out at the street. A kid on a scooter practised falling and getting up, which is the only leadership model that ever reliably works.

“I keep thinking ranks are both real and fake,” he said. “People act ranks. Families aren’t ladders. Stress makes ladders. The words float between all of it.”

“Both/and,” Joshua said. “Shop’s still open. Just don’t stock self-erasure under ‘loyalty’.”

After the call Michael wrote a short note and stuck it near the desk where later, heavier symbols would one day try to sit:

Alpha: parents, not tyrants.
Omega: not a permanent vacancy for your suffering.
If a role only exists because the cage is wrong, fix the cage—don’t decorate the collar.

He felt lighter, which was suspicious and welcome.

In the evening he declined one request that was really a transfer of someone else’s panic. He did it politely. No speech. No wolf metaphors. The other person sounded briefly surprised, then adapted, which is what adults do when the free shock-absorber goes offline.

The world did not end.

Which suggested, as data points go, that omega had never been a job description issued by reality—only a story some rooms told when they wanted a body to stand under the leak instead of mending the roof.

Michael slept without auditioning for the bottom rung.

The pack, such as it was, would have to share the weather.

Chapter 37

Is And Isn’t

747 words

It arrived as a noticing, not a revelation. Revelations hire brass bands. Noticings clear their throat and wait to see if you are paying attention.

Michael was at his desk after dark, the house finally quiet, two browser tabs open like rival pubs. One tab still held the wolf corrections—family, not ladder; captivity myths; Mech’s later honesty. The other held a plain page about the phrase people printed on jewellery and church walls: beginning and end, first and last, the pair of letters that bookend a claim about everything.

Alpha. Omega.

He said them under his breath and felt the double exposure click.

The words are hierarchy myth—still marching through gyms and comment sections, chin first, ladder proud.

The words aren’t accurate zoology for the wild animals they were stolen from—not as popularly sold.

The words are a sacred title in a book that likes monograms for the total.

The words aren’t finished with him yet; he could feel other meanings pacing just offstage, costumes not ready, cues not due.

Is and isn’t.

Both.

He opened the notes app and wrote the sentence the way you write a joke you must not rush:

α / ω is and isn’t.

Then, because Cameron lived rent-free in his quality-control department:

Not a formal proof. A clean noticing. Hold it lightly until the rest arrives.

His phone buzzed.

Joshua: You gone quiet. Productive quiet or spiral quiet?

Michael: Is and isn’t. Wolves and titles. Same letters, stacked transparencies.

Joshua: Good. Don’t force the stack into a single slide.

Michael almost sent a picture of the two tabs. Instead he sent:

Michael: The popular ladder is real as culture and false as science. The family reading is truer for packs. The big reading is… bigger. All of that can sit without one murdering the others.

Joshua: That’s the shop. Both/and without mush. False fork refused.

From Cameron, delayed, practical:

Cameron: If you start signing emails ‘from the Alpha and the Omega’ I will block you for taste crimes.

Michael: Noted. Working title for a thought, not a signature block.

He stood and stretched. The body, which is a conservative philosopher, reminded him that noticing still happens in a spine.

Is and isn’t—what a ridiculous, perfect engine. People wanted the world sorted into exclusive bins: true or false, myth or science, sacred or silly. The letters refused the bins. They behaved like light in the old experiments: wave if you ask wave questions, point if you ask point questions, rude to anyone who demanded a single costume for every party.

Michael walked to the window. Town lights. No single emblem. Multiplicity under whatever unity there was.

He thought of the caret and ampersand argument, of bedtimes that refine theories, of dialetheism as an optional door rather than a stolen verdict. This noticing belonged to the same family: not a court win against Cameron, not a demand that classical logic genuflect, just an accurate report from the desk where symbols kept arriving with more luggage than expected.

He wrote one more line:

Discovery beat, not decode. Leave the fonts alone for now.

Something in him wanted to rush ahead—to force the letters to confess every secret at once. He recognised the urge as the same all-or-nothing engine that turned job offers into cliffs. He let the urge pass through without giving it the steering wheel.

“Is and isn’t,” he said to the glass, and the glass steamed faintly, which is as close as windows come to applause.

Tomorrow he would still answer emails as a man with a name, not a monogram. He would still make breakfast. He would still fail at patience and retry.

But the pair of letters had stopped being only other people’s jewellery.

They had become a both/and machine with fur on one side and scripture on the other and a third side he refused to name yet, because timing is theology and suspense is just honesty about order.

He closed the tabs.

Before sleep he walked the short circuit of the house—landing, kitchen, back to desk—as if the body needed to ratify what the mind had filed. On the fridge, a child’s drawing of a wolf that was mostly eyebrows and teeth grinned with absolute confidence in the ladder myth. Michael grinned back.

“Is and isn’t,” he told the drawing. “You’re a family and a poster. Welcome to the shop.”

The thought stayed open.

That was enough for a night that had already paid its rent in attention.

Chapter 38

Fonts Waiting For The Universe

816 words

Monday, office hours of the self-employed, which is to say: pyjamas optional, concentration mandatory, the cursor blinking like a tiny impatient priest.

Michael was typesetting a slide for no good reason except that slides make thoughts feel official. He typed two Greek letters because they had been living in his head all weekend. He cycled fonts the way some people cycle radio stations.

Serif. Sans. Something pretending to be classical. Something designed for phones.

In one face the pair sat politely, museum-correct, the sort of shapes a textbook would approve.

In another they looked… livelier. As if the curves had developed opinions. As if adjacency had become a kind of body language.

Michael tilted his head.

He did not name what he almost saw. Naming would have been cheating, and also premature, and also the sort of move that turns a smile into a spoiler. He only registered the fact with the careful neutrality of a man noting weather:

Modern screens made some letterforms suggestive. Older calligraphy kept its hands to itself. The joke, if it was a joke, seemed to have been waiting for pixel densities and default typefaces the way a punchline waits for the right room.

“The universe waited for the right font,” he said aloud, then winced at his own grandiosity and amended: “Or people with dirty minds waited for the right font. Same empirical result.”

Joshua, on speakerphone while driving somewhere with potholes, made a noise of interest. “You’re typesetting theology again.”

“I’m noticing that symbols are not only meanings. They’re shapes. Shapes misbehave differently in different clothes.”

“Softly,” Joshua said. “You’re allowed to notice the clothes without ripping them off in chapter thirty-eight.”

“I know.” Michael changed the font back to something boring. The letters calmed down, like teenagers when a teacher walks in. “I’m filing it under setup. No speeches.”

Cameron messaged the group with psychic malice:

Cameron: Whatever you’re doing with Greek letters, if you’re about to overshare a visual pun I’m logging off for taste.

Michael stared, half convinced Cameron had installed a camera in the serifs.

Michael: No pun. Just fonts. Shapes change with typeface. Filing for later.

Cameron: Good. Later is my favourite theological category.

Michael saved the slide as do-not-present-yet.png and closed the file with the solemnity of a man locking a cellar that had started to hum.

He made coffee and thought about waiting.

Some truths are true in every century and only become visible when the tools catch up. Telescopes. Microscopes. Default fonts on a billion cheap screens. You could call it providence if you were in the mood. You could call it coincidence if you preferred safer furniture. Both/and could hold the mood without forcing a conversion at the kettle.

He remembered church walls with the same letters in gold leaf, dignified as furniture. He remembered gym posters with alpha in a typeface that wanted to start a fight. He remembered his own weekend notes: is and isn’t; mum and dad; not a job. Now the letters had added a fourth trick: appearance depends on medium.

That felt important and also slightly rude, which is often how important things arrive.

Joshua’s voice returned through a tunnel of road noise. “You still there?”

“Yes. Trying not to overclaim.”

“Overclaim would be ‘therefore the cosmos is a prank with a typesetting budget.’ Underclaim would be pretending you saw nothing. Middle path: the shapes are suggestive in common digital faces; meaning can wear more than one hat; we do not force the hats to marry before the wedding chapter.”

Michael smiled. “You’re getting good at this.”

“I’ve had practice not spoiling endings,” Joshua said. “Amen is a timing instrument.”

After the call Michael walked to the printer on impulse and printed the boring version of the letters, clean and chaste. Then he printed the lively version and folded it into the desk drawer without pinning it up.

The drawer clicked shut.

On the desk, only the polite pair remained, suitable for any audience, hiding nothing and revealing nothing, like a poker face made of vowels.

He wrote in the notebook:

Fonts wait. So can I.
Suggestive ≠ decoded.
Medium is part of the message’s mischief.

A delivery van reverse-beeped outside, pure industrial birdsong. Michael returned to actual work—the kind that pays for heating—and left the Greek alone.

But once or twice, unbidden, his eyes flicked to the drawer.

He thought of church gold leaf and gym posters and phone screens—three theatres, three costumes, same alphabet actors. Meaning was not only definition. Meaning was also what a shape did to a human animal who had a body and a history of jokes. That was not yet a thesis. It was a warning label on a sealed box: contents may shift in transit; do not open before the plot is ready.

The universe, if it was laughing, had the decency to do it quietly.

For now.

Chapter 39

Romans, Efficient And Ashamed

868 words

They met by the river again because some conversations need moving water to dilute them.

Michael had been thinking about crosses—not as jewellery, not as tribal badge, but as an object with a job description. Rome had been good at job descriptions. Roads. Law. Nails.

“I keep coming back to the engineering,” he said. “Vertical and horizontal. We already did God and neighbour. We already did the popular sermon with both beams. But the Roman version—”

Joshua waited. He was good at waiting when the sentence needed a longer runway.

“Efficient and ashamed,” Michael said. “That’s the product design. Death on the up-down axis—body failing in public gravity. Shame on the across axis—eyes, crowds, the social kill. One device, two punishments, no spare parts. Brutally optimised.”

Joshua’s face did the thing it did when theology arrived wearing work boots. “Go on.”

“I don’t need secret Roman mystics,” Michael said quickly. “I’m not claiming a lost committee memo from Golgotha Products Ltd. I’m saying: if you design a killing tool that also maximises humiliation, you have built both/and into the wood. Kill and display. End a life and unmake a face. Vertical dead. Horizontal watched.”

A rower slid past, neat as a sentence with good punctuation.

Joshua picked up a flat stone and did not throw it yet. “Connection inverted into disconnect. That’s what I keep hearing in it. The cross as public unbinding—person peeled off from community by spectacle. Then the later move: someone wears the shame and turns the whole apparatus into a sign of love that refuses to stay private.”

“Reinvention,” Michael said.

“Reinvention,” Joshua agreed. “Almost an inversion of the inversion. Shame → shamelessly public love. Not because pain is pretty. Because love refused to let empire keep the last authorship of the symbol.”

They stood with that. Gulls conducted committee meetings overhead.

Michael thought of his own smaller shames—the mask, the recruiter’s polished fork, the nights he had treated tired people as moral failures for having low bandwidth. Public and private humilations. The horizontal beam was not only ancient Rome. It was every room where eyes decide your worth.

“Does it help?” Joshua asked. “Or does it just hurt cleverly?”

“Both,” Michael said. “It helps because the both/and isn’t mush here. You can feel the load on each beam. Miss the death and you get sentiment. Miss the shame and you get a private spirituality that never risks being seen. The whole shape needs both, or it’s just a stick.”

Joshua finally skimmed the stone. It hopped twice, then surrendered to physics. “Church said God and neighbour for years. You sharpened it with Roman product design. I sharpened it with reinvention. Same shop, different shelves.”

Michael’s phone buzzed. Cameron:

Cameron: If you’re river-side theologising again, bring me into the punchline not the footnotes.

Michael: Romans: efficient death + public shame in one tool. Later: love steals the symbol.

Cameron: Grim. True enough. Still going to bed at a human hour.

Michael showed Joshua. Joshua grinned. “Patron sceptic clocks out. Theory improves.”

They walked. The path held joggers and dog-walkers and one man arguing with a headset as if empire had merely changed materials.

Michael said, “I used to think the cross was only vertical drama—me and the sky. Then only horizontal ethics—me and the queue. Holding both is harder. Holding death and shame at once is harder still. Holding love rewriting the shame is the bit that doesn’t fit in a spreadsheet.”

“Spreadsheets are bad at resurrection,” Joshua said. “They’re decent at logistics. Rome was logistics. The scandal is what happened to the logistics.”

Wind came off the water, honest and uninterested in conclusions.

Michael did not resolve the history into a tidy pamphlet. He did not need to. He had what the chapter required: a deeper cut of an old shape, filmable as two men by a river, one stone skipping, a phone with a friend promising sleep, and a piece of imperial carpentry that had been too good at its job and then—impossibly—repurposed by a story that refused to let efficiency and shame be the last word.

On the way back to the car Michael traced a small cross on the misted window with his finger—up, across—and wiped it away before it could become a performance.

He thought of Chapter Nine’s first pass at the beams—God and neighbour, death and shame, love’s later claim—and felt this afternoon as a second cut of the same timber, deeper, less introductory. The first time you see a tool you name its parts. The second time you feel what it was built to do to a person.

“Efficient and ashamed,” he said.

“And then,” Joshua said, “something else.”

“The something else is the bit people rush,” Michael said. “I’m not rushing. I want the Roman honesty first. Otherwise the love story becomes wallpaper.”

Joshua nodded. “Hold the shame long enough that the reinvention costs something. Otherwise it’s just a slogan with a plus sign.”

They left the river to its own both/and: fresh and tidal, dirty and alive, going out and coming in under the same bridges.

In the car Michael did not put on music. Silence after a true shape is a kind of respect.

Chapter 40

No Outside Left

838 words

The crisis did not arrive with thunder.

It arrived on a grey midweek afternoon while Michael was washing a plate, which is how the serious ones prefer to travel—under the cover of chores.

He had been stacking the week’s noticings without meaning to build a wall. Experience that would not unplug. Nothing that failed as an alternative. Rooms whose doors opened into more of the same here. Maps without a true edge. Systems that kept joining the museum of everything. Both/and as shop sign. Sandpaper friends. Optional logic doors. Masks. Forks. Wolves that were families. Letters that were and were not. Fonts with unfinished mischief. Wood that killed and shamed and then got stolen by love.

Individually they were thoughts.

Together they did something rude to the idea of elsewhere.

If there is no true outside—if every exit is still inside the only field—then where do you put the bin bag of the cosmos? Where do you throw the part you cannot stand? Where does hell live, if hell is eternal separation and separation cannot finish its paperwork?

Michael’s hands went still in the soapy water.

The feeling was not enlightenment. Enlightenment, in the brochures, comes with soft lighting. This felt like claustrophobia with a philosophy degree. Also—traitorously—like relief.

No outside left.

He dried his hands without drying them properly and walked to the back door as if fresh air could reinstall a beyond. The garden was small. The sky was large. Neither was elsewhere in the sense he meant. Sky was still experience. Garden was still here. Even the fantasy of escape was happening as a thought in a skull that had never found the hatch behind consciousness.

He texted Joshua a single line:

Michael: No outside left. Panicking politely.

Joshua rang within a minute. “Talk.”

“If there’s no true cut,” Michael said, pacing the kitchen tiles, “then there’s nowhere to put the rejected bits. No final exile. No clean hell as a separate estate. I’m stuck with the whole. We’re stuck with the whole. It’s—God, Josh, it’s airless and it’s the first breath at the same time.”

Joshua was quiet long enough to be useful. “Yes.”

“That’s not a comfort.”

“It’s not a slogan either,” Joshua said. “It’s a crisis because your old escape hatches were load-bearing. Sin-as-somewhere-else. Failure-as-outside-the-story. The fantasy that the unbearable can be relocated to a metaphysical landfill. If the landfill is still on the map of being, the rubbish doesn’t leave. It transforms the problem.”

Michael sat on the floor with his back against the cupboards, adult long legs folded like a bad camping chair. “I feel trapped.”

“You feel undivided,” Joshua said. “Trapped is what undivided feels like when you still wanted a trapdoor.”

Cameron messaged into the group, sensing weather:

Cameron: You two sound like a cult again. Translate.

Michael: If existence has no true outside, eternal separation is incoherent. Hell-as-exit fails. Also I can’t leave my own life by philosophy. Having a moment.

Cameron: Okay. That’s actually heavy. Still brush your teeth. Ontology doesn’t cancel dinner.

Michael laughed, helpless. The laugh cracked the airlessness just enough.

Joshua heard it. “There. Ordinary returns. Keep the crisis; lose the melodrama. No outside left means you don’t get to outsource repair. It also means nothing is finally thrown away from the field—including you when you fail, including them when they sleep, including the parts of the story that disgust you. The work gets more intimate. Not smaller. Closer.”

Michael looked at the bin in the corner—actual landfill’s little household apostle—and felt the metaphor try to get clever. He refused it. The point was simpler.

There was no balcony outside existence from which to judge existence.

There was only more here.

He said, “I’m scared of what that does to guilt. To blame. To the category people call sin.”

“Good,” Joshua said. “Scared means you’re not using it as a free pass yet. Next chapters can argue. Tonight you just survive the first sight of the closed map.”

After the call Michael finished the washing-up like a monk with a sink. He ate toast that was slightly burnt and called it dinner. He stood in the doorway of the bedroom and watched ordinary sleep happen without demanding it become a symbol.

No outside left.

Claustrophobic.

Free.

Both—because of course both—the shop never closed, even in a crisis.

He wrote three lines in the notebook and underlined the last:

Hell as permanent elsewhere: cannot complete.
Escape as metaphysical tourism: cancelled.
Only work left: here.

Then he turned off the kitchen light.

The dark was not outside the day. It was the day continuing by other means.

Michael went to bed inside the only room there is, and for the first time the phrase the only room felt less like a slogan and more like a sentence with consequences—consequences that would not stay polite, consequences that would start asking what sin even means when there is nowhere left to banish it to.

That question could wait until morning.

Morning, after all, would still be here.

Chapter 41

The Category Called Sin

763 words

Morning arrived without asking whether ontology was ready for it.

Michael stood at the kitchen counter with the same plate he had washed the night before and the same map that no longer had a beyond. Sleep had not reinstalled an outside. The crisis had only changed clothes: less panic, more paperwork.

If there was no true cut in the field of being, what happened to the old moral landfill?

He said the word carefully, as if it might bite.

Sin.

The category arrived with luggage. Hellfire posters. School corridors. Quiet rooms where people had been told they were stains. Also, unfairly mixed into the same suitcase: cruelty, betrayal, the things that leave other people with less room to breathe. He refused to throw the suitcase out unopened. Sorting was the job.

He rang Joshua while the coffee finished its small miracle.

“If there’s nowhere to banish anyone,” Michael said, “does sin stop existing, or does it just lose its favourite metaphor?”

Joshua did not rush the answer. Cars moved in his background; life continued to need indicators.

“Sin-as-standing-outside-the-whole can’t complete,” Joshua said. “That’s the paperwork failure. Harm doesn’t need that paperwork to be real. Cruelty is still cruelty when it happens inside the only room there is. Repair is still repair. The fantasy that fails is the clean exile—the idea that you can relocate the unbearable to a metaphysical elsewhere and call the remaining bit pure.”

Michael watched steam. “So I’m stuck owning the mess.”

“You’re stuck belonging to the same field as the mess,” Joshua said. “Including when the mess is you. Including when it’s them. No pure balcony. That’s heavier than a free pass. Anyone who hears ‘no outside’ as ‘no responsibility’ has failed the listening test.”

Michael: Translating for the group. Sin-as-exit fails. Harm still counts. No free pass.

Cameron: Good. I was about to write a very sharp text about people who discover monism and immediately become arseholes.

Michael: Please keep that text in draft forever.

Cameron: It’s laminated.

He walked the long way to the shop, not because revelation needed mileage, but because his legs wanted a vote. Along the path a man cursed at a delivery driver for existing in the same lane. The driver’s face did the small collapse of someone absorbing other people’s weather. Michael felt the old machinery try to fire: sinner, that one, put him outside the circle of the decent.

The machinery stalled.

The man was still an idiot. The driver still deserved better. Nothing about unity required Michael to pretend harm was a misunderstanding. What failed was the satisfying fantasy of a fence: pure here, impure there, landfill beyond the parish. The man’s meanness was happening in the same existence as the driver’s flinch and Michael’s judgment and the grey sky that did not take sides with a clipboard.

He bought milk. He held the door for a woman with a pram. He did not award himself a medal. Kindness that needs a medal is just branding.

At home he opened the notebook under last night’s three lines and added a fourth:

Sin-as-elsewhere: misfiled.
Harm-as-real: not optional.
Repair-without-exile: the actual work.

Joshua texted later: How’s the air?

Michael: Still no hatch. Less theatre. I keep wanting to put people outside and finding there’s only more inside.

Joshua: That’s the category cracking. Don’t replace it with mush. Keep the teeth. Lose the landfill.

Michael thought of private lists—apologies owed, sharpness he had called honesty, the small abandonments that do not make sermons and still thin a life. Those were not problems for a separate universe. They were work for this one.

He did not feel holy. He felt adult in the unglamorous sense: the sense that cancels the holiday of pure elsewhere and keeps the calendar of repair. Adult, here, did not mean joyless. It meant the jokes had to carry weight, and the weight had to allow jokes.

Evening, he washed the plate again. Same plate. Same hands. Same only room.

The category called sin, stripped of its exit fantasy, looked less like a cosmic stain chart and more like a set of verbs that still needed doing: stop, mend, refuse cruelty, tell the truth without enjoying the wound.

No outside left.

Therefore no sin-as-banishment.

Therefore—only therefore—more honesty about what still hurts people here.

He turned off the light. The dark did not exile the day. It continued it.

Tomorrow could argue about planks and eyes. Tonight the argument was simpler, and harder:

There was nowhere left to throw the parts of the story he could not stand.

Including himself.

Chapter 42

Verse Forty-Two

742 words

The chapter number arrived like a prop that had been waiting in the wings.

Michael noticed it on the notebook spine he had labelled in a fit of organisational optimism—Draft 42—and laughed with the particular embarrassment of a man caught by his own joke. Forty-two had already done duty as ordinary answer, towel philosophy, Cameron’s seatbelt. Luke had already loaned a plank. Now the calendar of the book itself was winking.

“Very funny,” he told the ceiling, which declined to take credit.

He made tea and opened the verse properly this time, not as a weapon for other people’s eyes, not as a badge of humility, but as a mirror he could not hire someone else to hold.

How can you say to your brother, “Brother, let me take the speck out of your eye,” when you yourself fail to see the plank in your own eye?

The comedy was structural. Carpentry props. Surgical pretence. A man offering optometry while wearing a forest.

Joshua came over mid-morning with pastries that were slightly crushed and therefore honest.

“Verse forty-two,” Michael said, and slid the notebook across.

Joshua read, then looked at the chapter label, then grinned. “The book is bullying you.”

“Deserved.” Michael tore a pastry. “I spent yesterday dismantling sin-as-elsewhere and nearly built a new superiority out of the rubble. Look how carefully I refuse exile. That’s a plank with better manners.”

“Planks love manners,” Joshua said. “Manners make them harder to see.”

They sat at the small table. Outside, a neighbour argued with a recycling bin about its destiny. Ordinary judgment, live demo.

Michael said, “Cameron’s forty-two and Luke’s forty-two keep shaking hands in my head. Adams: deflate the cosmic swagger. Luke: deflate the moral swagger. Same department, different uniforms.”

“And the chapter number?” Joshua asked.

“A reminder not to write a book that removes everyone else’s speck while I narrate my own enlightenment in soft lighting.”

Joshua raised his mug. “Amen, you forest.”

Michael: Chapter title is verse forty-two. I’m the plank exhibit.

Cameron: Finally, honest marketing.

Michael: You could pretend to be surprised.

Cameron: I could. I won’t. Also: if you start preaching Luke at people who need lunch, I’m reinstating the towel ban.

Michael: Lunch first. Optometry second.

Cameron: You’re learning.

After Joshua left, Michael did the least glamorous spiritual practice available: he listed three people he had recently corrected without first asking whether he was hungry, tired, or performing wisdom. One apology text went out. One stayed in drafts because timing was also ethics. One conversation needed a walk, not a sermon.

He walked.

On the path by the field the spaniel from the alpha week was winning again—sniffing, existing, ignoring a man who still believed dominance was a personality. Michael felt the itch to educate. The itch was a speck that thought it was a calling. He kept walking. The spaniel did not require his thesis.

At a café he overheard two strangers dissect a third who was not present. The dissection was skilled, even entertaining. He recognised the pleasure. It was the pleasure of being on the clean side of a fence. Yesterday’s chapter had tried to abolish the fence as ontology. Today’s verse abolished it as moral tourism.

He texted Joshua from the café window:

Michael: Plank inventory in progress. Speck tourism declining.

Joshua: Keep the inventory private enough to be true. Public plank-lists are just reverse vanity.

Michael: God, you’re annoying when you’re right.

Joshua: Brother’s office.

Evening, Michael sat with the verse and the ordinary number and the crushed pastry crumbs and felt the both/and without forcing a merger. Cameron’s forty-two still protected people from intensity cults. Luke’s forty-two still protected people from hypocrisy with a toolkit. The book’s forty-two was just a wink: you are not above the joke you are telling.

He almost texted the group a sermonette and deleted it. Public plank-lists, Joshua had said, are reverse vanity. The work was quieter: fewer corrections offered as gifts, more questions asked as if other people were real.

He wrote one clean line:

Before you reorganise anyone else’s eye, check whether you are wearing a forest and calling it clarity.

Then he washed two mugs, because discipleship that cannot manage crockery is mostly press release.

The dark came. Verse forty-two stayed open on the table like a friend who refused to flatter.

Michael left it there on purpose.

Some mirrors work better if you do not put them away too quickly.

Chapter 43

Four Twenty And Other Appointed Hours

744 words

At 16:20 the document saved itself with a little digital sigh, and Michael’s phone, which had opinions about everything, displayed the time in a font that made the joke unavoidable.

4:20.

He stared. Then he laughed, then he felt foolish for laughing, then he laughed at the foolishness, which is a kind of recursion the universe seems to enjoy.

He was not high. He was not in a dorm. He was a middle-aged man with a half-written chapter about moral landfills and a calendar full of meetings that did not care about cosmology. And still the clock had arrived like a comedian who knew the room.

Joshua answered on the second ring. “If this is an emergency, say emergency. If this is a sign, say sign and prepare to be teased.”

“Four twenty,” Michael said. “I hit save. The phone looked at me like a conspirator.”

“Appointed hour,” Joshua said, delighted. “Or confirmation bias with good comic timing. Both can buy a round.”

“I’m trying not to build a religion out of a digital clock.”

“Good. Religions built out of digital clocks tend to have terrible hymns.” Joshua’s voice softened. “But you can let it be funny without making it a master key. Humour is allowed to touch the sacred without annexing it.”

Michael walked to the window. School run weather. Someone’s child had drawn a sun with a face on a steamed bus glass; the face was already sliding into abstract art. The world kept appointing hours that meant nothing and everything depending on how hungry you were for meaning.

Cameron: Why did my ears burn. What have you done.

Michael: Clock said 4:20 while I saved a file about not inventing landfills for sin.

Cameron: Of course it did. The cosmos does stand-up for people who stare at it too long. Hydrate.

Michael: That’s your pastoral care?

Cameron: That’s my entire theology.

He made a list, not of omens, but of appointed hours the week had already offered without asking permission:

The night no outside left, while washing a plate.
The morning the sin category cracked.
The pastry hour of verse forty-two.
Now a stoner punchline wearing a wristwatch.

None of them proved a committee beyond the sky. All of them were available—moments when attention and event shook hands. You could call that God. You could call it pattern-hungry primates. You could call it both and go make dinner.

Michael tried the third option, which was becoming a habit.

He texted Joshua again:

Michael: Working rule: appointed hours are invitations, not warrants. I don’t get to arrest people with my coincidences.

Joshua: Frame that. Also: 4:20 is a fine time for tea if you refuse the other sacrament.

Michael put the kettle on like a man defusing a bomb with hospitality.

While it boiled he thought about people who lived by signs until the signs became a cage, and people who refused all signs until the world became a corridor of pure accident with no winks allowed. Both cages. The shop sign still said both/and: take the laugh, leave the tyranny.

At 16:21 the joke was already history. Clocks move on. That, too, was kindness. An appointed hour that overstays becomes a tyrant with a stopwatch.

He wrote in the notebook:

Synchronicity without sovereignty.
Winks without warrants.
If the universe tells a joke, you may laugh. You may not conscript the audience.

Joshua: Did the tea help?

Michael: Tea always helps. That’s not mysticism. That’s plant science and hot water.

Joshua: Same department as the rest of it, if you’re honest.

Evening traffic did its ordinary hymn. Michael closed the laptop before another round number could recruit him. He had work tomorrow that involved other people’s deadlines, not his private comedy club with time.

Still—walking the bin out after dark—he glanced at the phone and caught 20:04, which is 4:20’s quieter cousin if you are the sort of person who cannot stop seeing rhymes.

He put the phone away. The bin lid closed with a plastic certainty that no philosophy could improve. Somewhere a fox complained about municipal policy. The appointed hour of rubbish night continued without needing a doctrine.

“Later,” he told the night, which is a theological category Cameron approved of.

Later was not refusal. Later was pacing. The joke had been received; the audience had not been conscripted; the tea had been drunk; the workday still existed on the far side of sleep.

The night, being large, allowed it.

Chapter 44

Gold Tablets (Budget Version)

706 words

“I keep wanting to bury it,” Michael said.

They were in Joshua’s kitchen this time, which smelled of toast and the particular cleanliness of a house that had decided to be kind to its surfaces. Rain argued with the window. The argument was one-sided; windows are patient.

Joshua buttered toast with judicial calm. “Bury what?”

“The chain. The noticings. The no-outside thing. All of it. Put it in a box, put the box in the ground, invent a respectable story about angels and metal, come back later when I’m a different man with better lighting.”

Joshua handed him a plate. “Gold tablets, budget version.”

“Exactly. I can’t afford gold. I can afford a biscuit tin and a shovel and a myth about why the truth had to hide.”

They both knew the joke’s pedigree. Sacred history is full of finds, digs, plates, leaves, stones that wait under dirt until someone is ready—or claims to be. Michael was not mocking the hungry human need for a sealed deposit of meaning. He was confessing the temptation to outsource courage to archaeology.

“If it’s buried,” he said, “I don’t have to live it in traffic. I can be the discoverer later. Discoverers get documentaries. People who just wash plates with a new ontology get silence and crumbs.”

Joshua chewed, thought, swallowed. “Two problems. One: you’re already living it. The burial would be theatre. Two: gold-tablet stories often come with control systems—who may read, who may not, who holds the seer stone. You’re trying to escape a landfill for sin and accidentally building a locked cabinet for insight.”

Michael winced. “That’s uncomfortably neat.”

“I’m your brother. Neat is free.”

Michael: Confessed urge to bury the book like fake gold plates until I’m braver.

Cameron: Please do not start a religion in a biscuit tin. The planning permission alone.

Michael: It’s a metaphor.

Cameron: Metaphors dig too. Put the pages on a shelf like a normal person with a weird hobby.

Michael: That… is actually wisdom.

Cameron: I contain multitudes. Mostly caffeine and spite.

After lunch they walked in the rain with hoods up, looking like conspirators who had forgotten the conspiracy. A churchyard offered a shortcut. Wet stone. Names half-erased. The dead did not hand over tablets. They handed over the quieter fact of having been here.

“I don’t need metal,” Michael said. “I need permission to keep the draft aboveground where it can be wrong in public.”

“Permission from whom?” Joshua asked.

Michael almost said God, then almost said Cameron, then heard himself: “From the part of me that thinks unfinished truth is shameful.”

Joshua kicked a pebble along the path. “Unfinished is the only honest format for a living claim. Gold is for endings you want to freeze. You’re not finished. Neither is the field.”

They stopped under a yew that had seen worse weather and worse theologies.

Michael imagined the biscuit tin anyway—notebook pages, a cheap USB stick, a note saying for later—and then imagined rain finding the tin, rust negotiating with revelation, a fox digging for something less abstract. Comedy saved him. The budget version of sacred burial was just anxiety in a costume.

“I’ll keep it on the desk,” he said. “No dig. No angelic logistics. If it’s true, it can survive being seen by the cat.”

“The cat is an excellent peer reviewer,” Joshua said. “Unimpressed by everything.”

That evening Michael cleared a corner of the desk properly—not shrine, not archive, just space. He put the notebook there, open to the sin lines and the plank line and the appointed-hours rule. No lid. No lock. No story about future retrieval.

He wrote:

Gold tablets (budget version): refused.
Delivery method: ordinary air.
Storage: aboveground, correctable, slightly embarrassed.

Joshua: Still want a shovel?

Michael: Only for the garden. Beans, not scripture.

Joshua: Best hermeneutic I’ve heard all week.

Michael turned off the lamp. The notebook stayed visible in the dark like a small pale rectangle of unfinished nerve.

No angel arrived to congratulate him.

Something better happened: he felt the relief of not having to maintain a secret archaeology department in his own skull.

The truth, if it was a truth, could live where toast crumbs lived.

That was not less sacred.

That was the point.

Chapter 45

Goosebumps At The Sign

744 words

The sign was not mystical.

It was a road sign with a dent, a bus timetable bleached by sun, and—taped crookedly to a lamppost—a handwritten notice about a lost cat named Mabel who liked Dreamies and had a notched ear. Michael stopped because his body stopped. The hairs on his arms rose as if a small weather system had formed under his skin.

Goosebumps.

He waited for a voice. None came. The street continued being a street: someone dragging a suitcase on a broken wheel, a gull committing tax fraud on a chip wrapper, the smell of rain that had not yet decided to arrive.

Still the skin insisted. Data without a spreadsheet.

He photographed nothing. He did not need evidence for a trial he was not running. He stood with the sensation the way you stand with a song you cannot name—curious, slightly exposed, unwilling to recruit strangers into your private cinema.

Joshua: You went quiet again.

Michael: Goosebumps at a lamppost. Lost cat poster. No voice. Just skin.

Joshua: Somatic. File as contact, not command. Body often arrives before story.

Michael: I want to make it mean the chain is true.

Joshua: Of course you do. That’s the hungry bit. Let the goosebumps be goosebumps first. Meaning can apply for a visa later.

Cameron: If you start a ministry of arm-hair I will not be attending services.

Michael: It’s physiology.

Cameron: It’s also you. Hydrate, walk, don’t found a church on histamine.

Michael walked home the long way, arms still faintly electric. He remembered other skin-moments: cold floor on day one; the airless relief of no outside left; a hymn in a supermarket that had ambushed him between pasta sauces. None of them had issued bylaws. All of them had been real as weather.

At the kitchen table he tried an experiment. He thought deliberately of the landfill that failed, of sin-as-elsewhere cracking, of the ordinary number and the plank. Mild interest. Then he thought of nothing in particular and watched a patch of sunlight move across the floorboards like a slow blessing with no paperwork.

The goosebumps did not return on command. That was useful. Sacred skin is a poor servant of the ego. It comes when it comes, like cats and grace and good jokes.

He wrote:

Goosebumps are data.
Data is not automatically doctrine.
The body can say yes without writing a creed.

Later, at the corner shop, he saw another poster—same handwriting, Mabel still missing, a phone number soft with rain. He felt a smaller echo of the earlier charge, almost kindness rather than awe. Someone loved a cat enough to tape paper to metal in a windy city. That was already meaning enough for one afternoon.

He texted the group:

Michael: Lost cat posters might be the purest local theology. Appeal, description, hope, no army.

Joshua: Bring Mabel a Dreamie in spirit.

Cameron: If Mabel turns up at your door wearing a monogram I’m moving countries.

Evening, Michael stood under his own shower and felt ordinary water do ordinary work. No epiphany. Clean skin. The memory of lamppost weather remained like a stamp on an envelope already sent.

He did not need the goosebumps to prove the closed map. The closed map had arrived with plates and panic. The goosebumps were something else: the field touching the instrument that measures it from inside, briefly, without explanation.

Joshua rang once more, soft. “Still okay?”

“Yes,” Michael said. “Slightly ridiculous. Slightly grateful. Trying not to turn arm-hair into a proof.”

“Good man. Proofs that need goosebumps go missing when you have a cold.”

Michael laughed. “There’s a sentence for the back cover.”

“Don’t you dare.”

After the call he left a saucer of water in the garden for any cat that was not Mabel and might still be thirsty. It was not magic. It was manners toward a world that kept posting notices about love in the rain.

He checked the notebook once more and refused to add a forced moral. The day’s lesson, if it had one, was methodological: let the skin speak first; let the sermon apply later, if at all. Tomorrow he might walk past a doorway that was only a doorway until he counted it otherwise. Tonight was only arm-hair weather and a missing cat and a city that taped its hopes to metal.

When he slept, he dreamed no symbols.

He dreamed of a notched ear and a street that knew how to wait.

Chapter 46

A Doorway In The Ordinary

758 words

There was a doorway he had walked past for years without counting it as a doorway.

Not metaphor—wood and glass, a side entrance to a community hall that smelled of floor polish and old tea urns, the sort of place that hosted yoga on Tuesdays and arguments about the roof fund on Thursdays. A paper sign in the window said OPEN WHEN THE LIGHT IS ON. The light was on.

Michael paused with a carrier bag cutting into his fingers. Milk, bread, the small humiliations of needing to eat. He had not planned a pilgrimage. Pilgrimages that require planning often become content. This was only a door, and a light, and a body that had learned to notice when the street offered a hinge.

He went in.

Inside: stacked chairs, a piano with a broken smile, children’s artwork of impossible animals. A woman arranging leaflets looked up without alarm. “You all right, love?”

“Just… coming in,” Michael said, which was both inadequate and complete.

“Kettle’s boiled if you want one.” She nodded at a corner. “No charge. We pretend that’s economics.”

He took the tea. It was too strong and therefore perfect. He sat on a chair that wobbled and felt, without drama, the sensation of threshold: not leaving the world, not entering a separate heaven, just a pocket of ordinary consecrated by use. People had cried here. People had learned to stand on one leg here. People had held jumble sales for causes that would never trend.

Joshua: Where are you? You feel different over text, which is a ridiculous thing to type.

Michael: Community hall. Side door. Free tea. Light on means open.

Joshua: Doorway in the ordinary. Don’t annex it. Receive it.

Michael: I’m receiving tea. Does that count?

Joshua: That’s the advanced class.

A noticeboard offered bereavement support, a knitting group, a poster for interfaith conversation with a date already passed. Michael felt no urge to preach no-outside theology to the leaflets. The room was already practising something neighbouring: here is space; here is welcome; here is no ticket booth for worthiness.

Cameron: If you’ve joined a cult that meets beside a piano I’m sending sandwiches and a lawyer.

Michael: It’s a hall, Cam. Floor polish and broken animals in crayon.

Cameron: Acceptable. Bring me the crayon animals’ wisdom later.

The woman finished her leaflets and sat two chairs away, not crowding. “Quiet day,” she said. “Sometimes people only need the doorway. Not the programme.”

“That’s what I needed,” Michael said. “I think I’ve been trying to dig up gold plates when a side door would do.”

She smiled as if she heard stranger sentences weekly. “Side doors are underrated. Front doors perform. Side doors just work.”

He stayed twenty minutes. No vision. No monogram. No voice except the radiator’s opinionated clank. When he left, the light was still on for the next person. He put a couple of coins in a tin marked tea & biscuits and felt the economy of grace balance on a 50p piece.

Outside, the street had not become less itself. That was the gift. Sacred did not mean elsewhere. Sacred meant this, noticed, held open, light permitted.

At home he stood in his own kitchen doorway and tested the thought. Frame, hinge, threshold, ordinary air on both sides. No pure outside beyond the lintel—only more rooms of the same existence, some kinder than others, some needing repair, none located in a second cosmos.

He wrote:

Doorway ≠ exit from the field.
Doorway = redistributed attention inside it.
OPEN WHEN THE LIGHT IS ON — sufficient liturgy.

Joshua came by later with no agenda and sat in the garden while the sky performed grey. Michael told him about the hall, the tea, the woman who understood side doors.

“Local sacred,” Joshua said. “Doesn’t need your brand. You needed its chair.”

“I keep almost turning every hinge into a proof,” Michael said.

“Then practise walking through without collecting stamps.”

They sat until the air cooled. A neighbour’s radio offered three seconds of a song Michael almost knew, then static, then silence—another threshold, accidental, unowned.

“I used to think sacred places had to announce themselves,” Michael said. “Tickets. Architecture. A certain smell of importance.”

“Sometimes they smell of floor polish,” Joshua said. “Importance is often just marketing.”

When Michael closed the back door for the night he whispered thank you to the wood, quietly enough that even Cameron’s imaginary lawyer could not subpoena it.

The light in the hall, streets away, would go off eventually.

The category of doorway would not.

Chapter 47

Calls Into Being What Was Not

695 words

The verse arrived the way useful verses often arrive: not in a sermon, but in a half-remembered phrase that refused to stay half.

…God, who gives life to the dead and calls into being things that were not.

Romans. Paul in full rhetorical flight. Michael sat on the edge of the bed with a translation open on his phone and felt the sentence land in the week’s machinery like a missing gear.

No outside left. Sin-as-exit failing. Planks. Appointed hours. Refused gold. Goosebumps. A side door with free tea.

And now: calling into being what was not.

He rang Joshua before coffee, which is a love language and a warning sign.

“Talk me down or talk me through,” Michael said. “I’m not sure which.”

“Through,” Joshua said. “Down is for cliffs.”

Michael read the line aloud. The flat air received it without applause.

“I’ve been treating the closed map as only a crisis,” Michael said. “Claustrophobia with footnotes. But if there’s no outside, then novelty isn’t imported from a warehouse beyond being. Whatever becomes—kindness that wasn’t there yet, a sentence that rearranges a life, a repair—has to be called within. Life to the dead isn’t a delivery from next door. It’s the field refusing to treat dead ends as final furniture.”

Joshua was quiet, then: “Yes. Careful with the swagger, though. ‘Calls into being’ is not your personal manifestation board. It’s a claim about the total’s generosity. You participate. You don’t own the trademark.”

Michael exhaled. “I needed that slap.”

“It was a tap. You’re fragile this week in a productive way.”

They met by the river because moving water helped sentences that wanted to become empires. A rower cut a clean line. Somewhere a dog announced a philosophy of ducks.

Michael said, “When I write, something is not, then is. Paragraph from blank. That’s a tiny rhyme. When I apologise and a friendship warms, something that was not—trust—gets called. When I refuse the landfill for sin, a different moral world starts to exist in practice, not just in mood.”

“And when you hurt someone,” Joshua said gently, “you also call into being what was not—pain, distance, stories they’ll tell about you. The verb cuts both ways. Generativity isn’t automatically nice.”

Michael watched the water. “So the Romans line isn’t a free cuddle. It’s responsibility for participation in making.”

“Both/and shop,” Joshua said. “Comfort and warning in one aisle.”

Michael: Romans 4:17. Calls into being what was not. Filing under ‘closed map still does novelty’.

Cameron: If this becomes ‘I manifested a parking space’ I’m billing you for my attention.

Michael: It’s Paul, not a parking app. Making vs importing from Outside™.

Cameron: Fine. Still park like a citizen.

Afternoon, Michael opened a blank document and did not write the book. He wrote an email he had postponed—an offer of help, practical, unglamorous. Send. For a moment the help was only words. Then, hours later, a reply: thank you — that changes this week. Something that was not had been called, small as a domestic miracle, large as any.

He sat with the shiver of it—not goosebumps-at-the-sign, quieter. Efficacy. The field allowing rearrangement from inside.

Evening notebook:

Novelty without outside warehouse.
Call ≠ coerce.
Participation in making: holy and dangerous.

Joshua: Did you create a universe today?

Michael: I created an email. The universe consented to be slightly rearranged. Also I burned rice.

Joshua: Balance restored.

Before sleep Michael reread the verse once more and refused to tattoo it on his self-image. Paul could keep the megaphone. Michael would keep the practice: treat blank pages, cold relationships, and failed categories as sites where something not-yet might still be spoken into form—without pretending he was the only speaker.

He thought of cruelty that also calls into being what was not—and underlined Joshua’s warning in memory. Generativity without ethics is just power with better adjectives. The Romans line had to sit beside the cracked sin-category, not replace it with sparkle.

Outside, the town made night out of day, which is also a kind of calling.

Inside, he turned off the phone and let silence be a thing that was, fully, without needing to become a paragraph yet.

Chapter 48

Preference Without Outside

784 words

Quiet hour. Not the marketed kind with candles and a subscription box—just a gap between emails when the house held its breath and the fridge hummed like a monk with a sinus problem.

Michael sat with a question that had been pacing behind the week’s larger crises:

Why this?

Why this moment, this body, this particular angle of afternoon light on a chipped mug, rather than blank non-occurrence? If there was no outside warehouse of options, no committee room beyond existence where a clerk stamped APPROVED on reality, then presence was not a prize won against rivals. There were no rivals. There was only the field, preferring—somehow—to be here as this.

He did not dress the thought in robes. He let it be strange and almost tender.

Joshua arrived with nothing to fix, which is a rare gift. They sat. Steam rose. A van reverse-beeped somewhere with the confidence of a creature that believes beeping is personality.

“I’ve been thinking about preference,” Michael said. “Not ice cream. Ontological. If nothing can’t host a true alternative, then existence isn’t a coin flip against non-existence. It’s more like… the only game, and the game likes playing. This moment isn’t leftover. It’s what the total is up to.”

Joshua’s mouth tilted. “Careful. That can become narcissism—the universe prefers me specifically—or gratitude. Same wire, different voltage.”

“Gratitude track,” Michael said. “I’m trying. The claustrophobia of no outside is still real. But if the closed map is also the condition of any gift at all, then the trap is the gift’s shape. Preference without an outer stamp. No heaven’s HR department selecting me from a CV pile of void.”

Joshua nodded slowly. “I can pray with that. God not as recruiter from outside, but as the preferring itself—the is-ness that could not outsource its yes.”

They let the sentence sit until it stopped showing off.

Michael: Preference without outside. Why this moment if there’s no beyond to prefer from. Trying gratitude not main character syndrome.

Cameron: You are, unfortunately, a main character in your own skull. That’s anatomy. Just don’t bill the rest of us for your epiphany.

Michael: Fair. Also: tea is preferred. Solid data.

Cameron: Finally, science.

Michael refilled the mugs. Ordinary sacrament. He thought of the side door, the called-into-being email, the refused biscuit-tin burial. Each was a small preference enacted inside the only room: this way, not that, for now.

“Does this make suffering worse?” he asked. “If there’s no outside, pain isn’t a temporary visa from elsewhere. It’s native.”

“Native, and therefore not discardable into a metaphysical bin,” Joshua said. “That’s harsher and kinder. Harsher because you can’t exile it. Kinder because you can’t exile the sufferer either—not finally. The work stays intimate.”

Michael felt the week fold: sin category cracking, plank in the eye, clock jokes, gold refused, skin data, doorway, Romans, and now this soft engine—preference.
