Afterward, washing up, Michael felt the monogram stay in his wallet for once—present, not performing, not auditioning for the soup.

Parallel books, same story: the story of wanting to live without lying more than necessary.

He said that out loud. R. dried a bowl and said, ‘That version I’ll sign. Not with blood. With this tea towel.’

Cameron said, ‘Forty-two.’

Joshua said, ‘Amen.’

Outside the rain eased into something like forgiveness that did not require a ceremony. Inside, four people had not become one book.

They had become, for an evening, careful readers of each other’s weather—

—which is as close to a shared canon as the funny old life often needs.

Chapter 84

My Word Is My Bond

846 words

It began with a broken promise so small it could have been shrugged off: Michael said he would call a mutual friend back today, then didn’t, then felt the old grease of they’ll understand.

They would have understood. That was not the point.

He stood in the kitchen at ten at night, phone still dark on the counter like a quiet accusation, and heard his own voice from years of work deals and family spats—my word is my bond—sounding like a slogan he wore when convenient, like a suit for interviews. The sink held one mug. The mug did not care about slogans. The friend might.

He called. The friend was kind—busy day, no hard feelings, good to hear you. The kindness made it worse and better. He apologised without theatre, without turning the apology into a story about his interesting week of ontology. He asked about their mother. He listened to the answer. Bond begins in the unphotogenic.

Then he sat with the larger chain: red lines with R.; Christ == God said clean; seal and page distinguished; monogram not used as lasso. Words had been doing heavy lifting. If words were cheap, the whole bigger book was a draft written on water, and water does not hold signatures.

Morning, he met Joshua at the gym they both inconsistently attended. Treadmills faced a wall-mounted news channel muted, mouths moving like fish selling urgency. Sweat is a truth serum for men who talk too well when dry.

‘I want word-as-bond to be structural,’ Michael said between breaths. ‘Not branding. If I say I’ll respect a red line, that’s covenant. If I say double-equals, I don’t dilute it in another room to be liked. If I say parallel not capture, I don’t capture when I’m alone with my diary. If I say I’ll call, I call.’

Joshua wiped his face with a towel that had seen better decades. ‘Bond means cost. A word that never costs is a weather vane. Where will it cost you this week? Name the bill before it arrives.’

Michael thought, feet pounding cheap rubber. ‘There’s a meeting where people will reward me for a clever synthesis of “all faiths.” I will not perform R. as evidence. There’s a chat where I could win by sarcasm. I will pay the points—lose the thread-win. There’s a yes I want to say to look generous. I’ll say no if I can’t carry it.’

‘Good. Tell Cameron. Public bonds to friends are harder to weasel. Weasels hate witnesses.’

Cameron, informed by voice note while Michael stretched a hamstring that argued back, replied in text: I’m logging this. If you breach, I collect one humiliating anecdote at a time of my choosing. Also: word-as-bond includes not promising cosmic things you can’t deliver. Stay mammal.

Michael: Fair. Mammal.

R., when he stated it without flourish over coffee—my word to you is bond; red lines included; no soft conversion project even in my head if I can help it—studied him as if checking for counterfeit ink.

‘I’ll hold you to it,’ she said. ‘Not as police. As someone who knows what it costs when men’s words are décor. In my life, oaths matter. You don’t have to use my forms. You do have to mean yours. If you slip, repair fast. Slow repair is just another broken word wearing shoes.’

‘I mean mine. If I slip, I repair.’

‘Then we continue.’

That week he practised small bonds: arrived when he said; declined what he could not carry; corrected a story he’d exaggerated about a work win in a group chat (I overstated; here is the duller truth); called the mutual friend again just to be a presence, not a penitent. Each act felt unphotogenic. Each act made the monogram less of a costume and more of a private joke that didn’t need an audience.

Friday evening Joshua poured tea and said, ‘You’ve been quieter online.’

‘Bond uses fewer posts,’ Michael said. ‘I’m trying not to spend language like confetti. Confetti is pretty. It also means someone else sweeps.’

Joshua grinned. ‘Look at you. Almost forty-two.’

Michael thought of Cameron and smiled. ‘Don’t tell him. He’ll get smug.’

‘Too late,’ Joshua said. ‘Smug is his cardio. He’s been training for this moment since the systems week.’

Before sleep Michael wrote on paper, not phone:

If the field is one, lying still injures the only room we’re in.
Word is bond because there is no second universe to dump the falsehood into.
Repair is part of bond, not the opposite of it.

He folded the paper beside the napkin without letting them marry. Two documents, one wallet, no merger.

Lights out. Harriet’s breathing from the other room. Outside, a car door. Inside, a man who had called when he said he would—at least once—which is how civilisations begin again each night.

His word, for once, stayed in the room after he stopped speaking—

—which is the only bond the funny old life actually enforces, and it enforces it by the look in a friend’s eyes when you show up or don’t.

Chapter 85

NRE And The Brain's Last Cinema

802 words

Cameron named it first, because Cameron collected acronyms that deflate romance.

‘NRE,’ he said, feet on Michael’s coffee table in the allowed way. ‘New Relationship Energy. Usually people. Also jobs, gyms, gods, monograms. Brain chemistry throws a festival. You think you’ve found the Real. You’re also on a free trial of dopamine with a hard end date. Read the terms. There are no terms. That’s the trick.’

Michael, who had been about to describe the monogram week as pure epistemic light, shut his mouth, opened it, shut it again. A biscuit tin sat between them like a neutral country.

Joshua, from the armchair: ‘He’s been glowing. I liked the glow. I also like livers that process toxins. Glow without liver is just fever with better branding.’

Michael sat on the floor, back against the sofa. ‘You’re saying α==ω was a crush.’

‘I’m saying it can be true and a crush,’ Cameron said. ‘Both. Your brand, ironically. NRE makes you write poems on napkins and ignore sleep. It also makes you a missionary with glitter. The cinema in the skull runs highlight reels—montage of cold floor, pub, fonts, R.’s face, the song at 06:08. Last cinema of the night—everything feels edited for destiny. Destiny is a bad editor. It cuts the washing-up.’

R. had been invited for this one; she arrived mid-metaphor with biscuits that were not metaphorical. ‘Are we pathologising his revelation? Because if this is only “Michael had feelings,” I will leave and take the biscuits. Feelings aren’t fake. They’re also not the whole court.’

‘Not only,’ Cameron said carefully, feet coming off the table as if manners had been sworn in. ‘Regulation. When I fell in love I also correctly noticed my partner existed. The noticing was true. The soundtrack lied about permanence of intensity. Keep the noticing. Renegotiate the soundtrack.’

Michael took a biscuit as if it were medicine. Crumbs on his shirt. ‘How do I keep the true without marrying the soundtrack? I don’t want to throw out the monogram because my brain threw a party.’

Joshua held up fingers. ‘Sleep. Ordinary tasks. Sandpaper friends. Revisit the claim when you are bored—genuinely bored, not performing boredom. If it only lives at 06:08 with metal ballads, it’s a scene, not a spine. If it survives bin night and a dull Tuesday email, keep it. If it needs constant novelty to feel real, it’s NRE wearing a halo.’

R. nodded, pouring tea she had not been offered because she had brought her own thermos like a professional. ‘In religious life we know ecstasy. We also know the morning after ecstasy when the dishes are still dirty. I won’t sneer at your heat. I will ask what you do when the heat cools—kindness or abandonment of the insight? Heat that only loves the high will betray the low. The low is where friends live.’

Michael thought of the first cold floor, months of chain, the pub with the empty chair, the red lines, Denise’s jam, Harriet’s feet on his lap. Heat had been real. So had work. So had apology.

‘NRE on God,’ he said slowly, ‘tempts me to treat people as supporting characters in my biopic. That’s the danger. The cinema casts extras badly. Extras have names. Denise. Carol who doesn’t call enough. The man at dinner who hated the monogram. They are not NPCs for my highlight reel.’

Cameron pointed, triumphant without cruelty. ‘There. That’s the sentence that earns more biscuits. Have two.’

They made a practical pact written on the back of an envelope: for two weeks Michael would not make any major relational or public-theological moves after 11 p.m.; he would run claims past at least one unimpressed friend; he would schedule dull joys—swimming, accounts, calling his mother about nothing, bin night with presence.

‘Romance of the absolute meets spreadsheet,’ Joshua said. ‘Holy. Also floss. Horizontal beam includes gums.’

That night the brain tried to open the cinema anyway: montage of napkin, ridge path, stadium lights, keys six and eight, R. saying guest not ingredient. Michael watched one reel, then got up and washed a plate. The plate was not in the montage. The plate was true. Soap, water, a chip of pasta from two nights ago. He dried it. He put it away.

He texted the group: NRE named. Ego sulking. Insight still present on bin-night test. Going to bed like a mammal.

Cameron: Proud. Forty-two. No 11 p.m. revelations. I’ll know.

R.: May your mornings be less cinematic and more honest.

Joshua: Amen to unimpressive dawn. Sleep.

Michael slept. Somewhere the last cinema closed its doors without locking them—tickets still available, but not required for entry into tomorrow—

—and the funny old life, which includes chemistry and chalk and love, waited to see whether he could recognise without the free trial soundtrack cranked to eleven.

Chapter 86

Cyclical Realities

876 words

The washing machine entered spin and Michael’s thoughts, which had been pretending to be a straight corridor with a trophy at the end, went round with it.

Glass, water, shirts. A sock flashed past like a minor prophet with no congregation and too much velocity. Final chapters, bigger books, seals, open pages—all of it had been living in his head as a hallway you walked once and then graduated from. The drum suggested otherwise: same cotton, new dirt, clean again, dirt again. No certificate. Only soap and gravity and the ugly honesty of a programme called cotton 40.

Joshua arrived with croissants and found him standing there with tea gone cold, face lit by the little window of the machine.

‘You’ve been hypnotised by laundry,’ Joshua said. ‘I respect that. Laundry never lies about how long it will take.’

‘Cycles,’ Michael said, still watching the sock. ‘I keep treating recognition like a once-for-all graduation. Then Tuesday arrives and I’m jealous of a stranger’s parking space, or petty about the dishes, or tired in a way that makes me cruel, and I think I’ve fallen out of the truth. What if the truth includes the spin cycle?’

Joshua put pastry on a plate, brushed flakes onto the counter, and refused to apologise to the counter. Crumbs were evidence of eating, not failure of hospitality.

‘Linear pride hates cycles,’ he said. ‘It wants a conversion date and a flat graph of improvement that only goes up and to the right, like a share price designed by an optimist. People are seasons. Faith is seasons. Even funny old life has weather, not a permanent climate-controlled revelation with complimentary robes.’

They ate standing up. Outside, rain started, stopped, started again—the sky doing laundry without shame. Something in Michael’s shoulders loosened: permission to relearn kindness without being expelled from his own life.

Later they walked to the park because R. had ten minutes between errands, which is how real friendship often schedules metaphysics—a bench, a bus timetable, no retreat centre. A dog chased the same stick into the same shallow river with undiminished joy, shook itself, and waited for the throw again as if novelty were a rumour invented by people with money.

Michael pointed. ‘That dog is my spiritual director today.’

R. smiled, scarf tucked, bag at her feet. ‘Islam has a calendar that returns,’ she said when he floated the spin cycle properly, not as poetry but as a problem he had been having with himself. ‘Fasts return. Feasts return. You don’t graduate from needing the year. Christians have liturgical rounds too, when they remember. The danger is thinking cycle means nothing matters—hamster wheel, shrug forever. Cycle can mean keep practising because you are a creature. Creatures get dirty. Creatures wash. Creatures do not get a permanent diploma that exempts them from Tuesday.’

She checked the time—not as insult, as creaturehood. ‘I have to go. Same bench next week if the weather allows. That’s a cycle too. Don’t make it a shrine. Make it a habit that can survive rain.’

When she left, they watched the dog complete another lap of pure, unembarrassed repetition, then took the long way home along the canal. The water was never the same water. The path was the same path. A runner lapped them, breathing like a metronome that had found religion. Both true: change and return.

At his desk he wrote nothing grand. He paid a bill he paid every month. He stretched the hip that complained when he pretended he was twenty-five. He watered the plant he usually forgot until it staged a crisis. He did not ask the monogram in his wallet to be new. He asked it to be again—usable on a dull afternoon when no song was playing and no one was watching.

Evening, Joshua phoned while Michael folded shirts still warm from the machine.

‘How’s the cycle?’

‘Less shame when I relearn the same kindness,’ Michael said. He shook a sleeve by the cuff, ordinary ritual. ‘I used to think repetition meant I failed the final exam. Now it might mean I’m alive. Also I still snap at bad drivers. Recognition didn’t delete me.’

‘Good,’ Joshua said. ‘Deleted people can’t love. Slightly damp people can. Amen to damp.’

Before bed Michael put another load on—towels this time, the humble cloud. The machine sang its ugly song. He stood with one hand on the plastic lid and thought of α==ω not as a finish line but as a shape you could walk around year after year, seeing both readings depending on the light, forgetting, remembering, washing the same mug.

He did not take notes. He turned the dial and pressed start, which is a kind of amen if you do it without an audience.

The towels tumbled. Outside, a fox crossed the road with freelance confidence and vanished into a hedge that had seen every season and would see them again. Michael switched off the kitchen light and left the machine to its work—round and round, clean enough for another day of ordinary dirt—

—and for the first time in weeks, the corridor in his head stopped insisting on a trophy and started looking like a path that came back to the same sink with slightly cleaner hands.

Chapter 87

I Was You And You

750 words

The sentence came out of Michael’s mouth before he had filed a risk assessment.

They were on the ridge again—R.’s preferred courtroom—after a week of careful categories. Wind edited hair and speeches. A younger man on a bench was arguing with someone on the phone in the universal key of you never listen. His free hand chopped the air into paragraphs. Michael heard his own past voice in the stranger’s throat—the year he had been sure volume equalled truth.

‘I was you,’ he said quietly to R., nodding that way. ‘Different year. Same heat. And you were me when I was sure my map was the forest.’

R. followed his gaze, then looked back. ‘Careful. “I was you” can be empathy. It can also be colonising someone’s moment with your memoir.’

‘Theory of mind with a time machine,’ Michael said. ‘Not capture. Recognition that the fool and the preacher and the tired nurse cycle through one field. I don’t stay the hero of my own biopic. I rotate.’

She sat on the other end of the bench from the phone argument, distance as kindness to a stranger mid-storm. ‘Say the full line you’re chewing. Out loud. Where I can catch it if it goes wrong.’

He breathed. Grit under his shoe. ‘I was you and you were me. Not identity. Permission to stop inventing a separate species called them. When I was sure atheism was adulthood—I was you, if you’re sure now about something else. When you were patient with a stupid question at Scripture Reasoning—you were me when Joshua was patient. When Cameron went to bed instead of crowning me— I have been the man who needed bed and refused it. The loop is moral. It blocks contempt.’

R. watched the kite of some distant child jerk and recover. ‘I can receive that if it makes you gentler with the man on the phone and gentler with me when I frustrate you. I reject it if it means you think you’ve already lived my faith from the inside because you once had a phase.’

‘Rejected that version,’ Michael said immediately. ‘Phases aren’t initiation. I was you only in the human weather—fear, pride, love, the wish to be right. Not in your prayer. Not in your law. Not in your mornings I don’t attend.’

‘Good. Keep the weather. Leave the house keys.’

The man on the phone said fine in a way that meant not fine and hung up. Silence after anger is also a cycle. He stared at the view as if the city owed him an apology. Michael did not approach with wisdom. He had been that stare.

Joshua, when told later over video while he cooked for someone else’s children, wiped an eye with the back of a wrist and nearly lost a wooden spoon into the pan. ‘That’s the shame-into-love engine in civilian clothes. If I was the one who hurt and the one who healed, contempt loses its visa.’

Cameron: Fine. Just don’t do “I was you” at people mid-crisis. They need water, not your novel. Save the loop for after the ambulance or the apology.

Michael put that in the notes under ToM, then added: Loop ≠ speech. Loop = inner visa check before contempt.

On the bus home a teenager scowled at an old man’s slowness boarding. Michael felt the scowl from both sides of time: knee that would stiffen; impatience that thought it was free. He stood and offered the old man his seat without a sermon. The teenager looked away, possibly learning, possibly not. Michael smiled at no one in particular, which is how you pray without alarming Transport Police.

He texted R.: I was you / you were me — weather only. Bond still holds.

R.: Weather accepted. Don’t forget umbrellas. Also thank you for not preaching at the phone man.

Night, at the kitchen table with Harriet half-watching a show about people renovating houses as if walls were confessions, he wrote the line once, clean:

I was you and you were me — not to erase, to end the fantasy of a separate moral planet.

Harriet glanced over. ‘That one I like. Use it on yourself first when you’re angry at me for existing.’

‘Bond,’ he said, and closed the book without posting anything.

The funny old life, which rotates fools and saints through the same bones, left the stranger on the ridge uncollected—

—still free to be someone else’s present tense, not a prop in Michael’s improved autobiography.

Chapter 88

Flood And The Child Held

765 words

Sunday, Joshua asked for help at the community centre soft play: too many children, not enough adults with knees still under warranty.

Michael went in old jeans and a shirt he did not love. R. came for an hour between family obligations, scarf practical, smile ready. Cameron sent a gift card for juice cartons and a note: I am present as infrastructure.

The room was loud in the way oceans are loud if oceans ran on sugar. Foam blocks in primary colours. A ball pit that smelled of socks and optimism. A laminated rules poster nobody read until blood was theoretical.

Michael’s job was simple: be a safe wall. Lift the toddler who wanted the slide and feared the slide. Clap without irony. Wipe a nose with the competence of men who have decided dignity includes snot. He learned a child’s name—Aisha—and used it, because names are anti-flood technology: they pick one face out of the water.

In a lull he stood by the window with a small girl on his hip—someone’s niece, temporarily universal—while her father dealt with a sibling crisis involving a shoe. The child rested her head under his chin with the absolute trust of people who have not yet read philosophy. Her hair smelled of baby shampoo and the outside air.

Flood stories rose unbidden: water, fear, a vessel, what you carry when the world will not stay polite. Arks and promises and the old terror of too much. He did not tell the child a flood story. He held her. His arm shook slightly; he adjusted. Holding is a practice, not a pose.

R. appeared at his elbow with two paper cups of water. ‘You look like a Renaissance painting titled Man Surprised By Horizontal Beam.’

‘I’m terrified of dropping anything precious,’ Michael said.

‘Good terror,’ she said. ‘Keep it. The bad terror is thinking your ideas will catch them if your arms fail.’

Joshua herded a conga line of four-year-olds past the ball pit. His face was lit with the particular joy of service that does not need an audience. Michael understood, not for the first time, that amen could be a body between a child and a hard floor. A boy tripped; Joshua’s hand was there before drama could patent the moment.

When the girl’s father returned, grateful, ordinary, Michael passed her over and felt the weight leave his arms like a tide going out. The absence was its own theology. He flexed his fingers. Pins and needles. Incarnation has circulation problems.

Later, stacking foam blocks into a wall that immediately became a fort, he said to R. and Joshua: ‘Flood as image—everything too much. What you hold above the water is not your cleverness. It’s the next vulnerable thing. The monogram doesn’t float a child. Arms do.’

Joshua nodded, breathing hard from the conga. ‘No-sin-as-outside doesn’t mean no duty. It means the child is not outside God when she cries. So you show up.’

R. set a block carefully on another. ‘In my stories of water and mercy I won’t give you a free comparative essay today. I will say: holding matters more than winning the metaphor. Also your knees are loud. Sit when you can.’

Cameron’s juice arrived via a volunteer who looked confused about divine payment systems. They poured for thirsty small animals in human form. Aisha wanted purple. Purple was allowed. The world did not end.

On the walk to the car park Michael’s shirt stuck to his back. R. left for her next obligation with a wave that meant continue. Joshua locked the centre’s side door with a key on a comic keyring—plastic dinosaur, no theology required.

Home that evening, Michael showered the sticky off and stood a long time with water louder than thought. Flood as cleansing, flood as threat, flood as the day becoming too much noise—and through it, the simple fact: he had held a child, and the field had not required a napkin for that to count.

He texted Joshua: Arms > monogram today.

Joshua: Both, when the child is asleep. Today arms. Amen.

R.: Proud of your knees.

Harriet, when he described the weight on his hip, said, ‘That’s the book I want you writing,’ and handed him a plate without further exam.

Michael slept early.

In a dream he might have floated an ark; he did not remember.

He remembered the weight on his hip, real as cold laminate, sacred as any seal—

—and the funny old life, which includes soft play and snot and rescue without drama, let that be enough scripture for one wet Sunday.

Chapter 89

Things Are Not Always The Same

861 words

Unity had a hangover risk: the urge to say same until difference died of smothering.

Michael noticed it mid-sentence on a work call, which is a rude place for theology to ambush you. A colleague was struggling—voice flat, tired, the particular fatigue of people who have been told to be resilient as if resilience were a free app. Michael had been about to say we’re all in the same boat when the colleague said, without drama:

‘Actually, my boat has a hole and yours has a motor, so please don’t.’

Silence on the line. Office noise in the background like a sea that had not chosen a side. Somewhere a printer chewed paper with the commitment of a small animal.

Michael shut up. He listened. He took notes that were practical—deadlines that could move, a contact in HR, an offer to cover Thursday’s meeting. He helped without poetry. After the call he walked the block swearing softly at his own universal solvent, the way it dissolved other people’s specifics into a warm bath called Oneness and congratulated itself for being kind.

He called the emergency council: Joshua in person on a park bench that had lost a slat, R. on speakerphone, the wind doing its best to edit everyone.

‘I’ve been drunk on one field,’ Michael said. He sat on the good side of the bench, the side that still believed in backs. ‘No outside is true for me. Then I treat pain as interchangeable. Things are not always the same. Parallel books, not identical books. Different holes. Different motors.’

R.’s voice came clear over the wind. ‘Thank you. When people say “same” too fast, my history disappears. So does disability. So does money. Unity that erases is just empire with yoga pants.’

Joshua kicked a pebble into a puddle. The puddle accepted it without a sermon. ‘Both/and includes and different. The cross had two beams, not one soup. Horizontal means neighbour as neighbour—not as your mirror. If Denise’s boiler and your monogram are “the same,” you’ve lost the plot and the wrench.’

Michael laughed without fun. ‘I almost baptised a hole into a metaphor to make myself comfortable. Comfort is a bad priest.’

They worked examples like first aid, Joshua counting on fingers:

‘Same: both need respect. Not the same: who gets stopped by security.’

‘Same: both can be wrong. Not the same: whose wrong gets a newspaper.’

‘Same: both die eventually. Not the same: who dies early from policy.’

R. added, quieter, ‘Your monogram can stay generative without saying every body is the same body. Generation itself depends on difference. Ask a biologist. Ask anyone who has ever needed a particular medicine. Ask me when you almost made my seal your open page’s twin.’

Michael looked at his hands—ordinary, uninjured, free to type, free to walk a block while angry at himself, free to leave a meeting early because no one would dock his dignity for it. The wind found the gap in his jacket and made its case.

‘I’ll practise not the same as a holy phrase this week,’ he said. ‘Alongside one field. If that tensions, good. Tension means I haven’t flattened the map into a poster next to the fridge magnets of enlightenment.’

Joshua slung an arm around his shoulder. The bench creaked in solidarity. ‘Welcome back to edges inside the whole. Edges aren’t outsides. They’re how shapes work. How boats float. How jam jars have labels so you don’t spread apricot on a wound that needed something else.’

That evening Michael sat at the kitchen table and forced a notebook column headed NOT THE SAME with concrete names—not abstractions, not fields, not the soft blur of cosmic goodwill. People he loved. People he had failed. Systems with teeth. The colleague’s boat, as named by the colleague, not as rebranded by Michael’s need to feel spacious.

Harriet came in, hung her bag, glanced at the page without reading it like a spy.

‘You look like a man who has been told off by reality,’ she said.

‘I have. Reality was right.’ He closed the notebook. ‘Tea?’

‘Always. Don’t explain the ontology of kettles.’

He did not. He made tea for two, handed her the mug with the chip she preferred because it fitted her hand, and only later messaged the colleague: You were right about the boats. Motor is yours to name. Help is mine to offer without poetry.

The reply was brief and kind: Thanks. Poetry later maybe. Today just the hole.

Michael put the phone face down. Outside, a neighbour dragged a bin that squeaked like an argument settling. Inside, Harriet told him about a difficult email and a kind stranger at the chemist, and he asked follow-up questions that had nothing to do with unity and everything to do with her day.

Things are not always the same.

The recognition did not die. It grew a spine, and the spine had room for particular leaks, particular motors, particular faces that refused to be melted into a warm bath—

—and love, for once, looked less like blending and more like showing up with the right tool for a hole that was not a metaphor.

Chapter 90

Harriet At Six Thirty-One

800 words

The clock on the cooker said 18:31 when Harriet put two plates on the table and said, “You’re here. Good. Eat before you invent another symbol.”

Michael blinked. Home had a way of demoting prophets to men who should wash their hands.

Harriet was not a subplot. She was the horizontal beam with shopping bags and a memory for which friends were allergic to what. She had survived his intensity seasons before—the late nights, the sudden vows, the mornings when he spoke like a man translating from a language only he could hear. She had also told him, more than once, to take the bins out, and had not found the instruction mystical.

“I was with Joshua and the soft play,” he began, pulling out a chair that scraped in a familiar key.

“I know. He texted a photo of you looking terrified with a toddler. I liked that version of you.” She poured water. Condensation ran down the glass like a small honest weather system. “How’s R.?”

“Respected. Not solved. Red lines holding.”

“Good. Solving people is a vice.” She sat. The plates held food that looked like food, not like a metaphor—vegetables with actual colour, something that had met an oven. “How’s the cosmic joke?”

He almost reached for the wallet napkin. He didn’t. The napkin could survive dinner without being exhibited. “Still true. Less cinema. Cameron named NRE. I am trying to be a mammal with a job and a kitchen.”

Harriet’s mouth softened. “I need the mammal. I can love the thinker. I will not date a loudspeaker that forgets to ask how my day was.”

“How was your day?” he asked, on time, and meant it enough to put his phone face down without checking whether meaning had been received by the universe.

She told him—work, a difficult email that made her jaw tight, a kind stranger at the chemist who had helped with a bag when her hands were full, the ordinary epic of a day that did not need Greek to be real. He listened without filing her sentences under his chain. When he caught himself mapping, he stopped and asked a follow-up about the chemist’s accent instead, and she laughed, and the laugh was better theology than half his notes.

They ate. The food was good in the way food is good when someone else wants you alive. Forks clicked. Outside, a neighbour’s television leaked laughter through a wall.

At 18:31 still glowing in his peripheral vision—cookers lie about time with confidence—he thought of 06:08 and Unfaithful and keys and seals and floods. All of it could wait for the second helping of vegetables.

“I don’t need you to believe the monogram,” he said carefully. “I need you to believe I’ll do the washing-up.”

Harriet considered him over the rim of her glass. “Washing-up is a creed I can share. Show me the monogram if you want; don’t make it my homework. And if God is as funny as you say, God can cope with us watching something stupid on the sofa after.”

“Deal.”

They did the stupid sofa thing. Her feet found his lap with the authority of long affection. On screen, people pretended to cook competitively under lights that would make anyone’s sauce look ambitious. In the room, two lives shared heat without a thesis defence. Michael noticed he was not narrating the scene to an imaginary reader. That felt like recovery.

Joshua rang once; Michael let it go to voicemail, then texted: Harriet 18:31. Horizontal. Call tomorrow.

Joshua: Amen. Tell her I still owe her a cake.

Harriet, reading over his shoulder with the shamelessness of cohabitation: “He does. Double chocolate. I’m not a metaphor, Josh.”

Michael typed that. Joshua replied with a humbled emoji and a promise that, for once, Michael intended to help him keep.

Later, lights low, Harriet said into the quiet: “You’re kinder this week. Keep the source if the source is kindness. Bin it if the source is being right.”

“That’s the quality bar,” Michael said. “You and R. should form a council.”

“We might,” she said. “You’ll be terrified. Healthy.”

He washed the plates while she slept halfway on the sofa, one arm flung as if sleep were a sport she still won. Soap. Warm water. The small competence of clean ceramic. 18:31 had become 21-something. Time cycled. The bond held.

Harriet at six thirty-one had not replaced the monogram.

She had located it—inside a life where love had a face, a plate, a rule about bins, and a sofa that could hold a man without requiring him to be interesting every minute—

—and the funny old life, which ends chapters and still wants dinner, nodded from the kitchen doorway like a friend who knows when to stop talking and start drying up.

Chapter 91

Horizontal Beam With A Face

696 words

The neighbour’s name was Denise, and Michael had spent two years calling her the neighbour as if anonymity were politeness.

She knocked because her boiler had chosen a dramatic death on a Thursday evening and she needed a pair of hands and a lift to the hardware place before it closed. Harriet was on a late shift. Michael had been mid-sentence in a note about capstones. He said yes before his monogram could hold a meeting about meaning, put shoes on wrong-footed, fixed them at the door.

In the car Denise talked about her grandson, her hip, the price of bread, a sister in another city who didn’t call enough. The heater smelled of dust burning off. Michael listened. When he spoke, he asked about the grandson’s school play—sheep, apparently, with a speaking line. He did not mention fonts. He did not mention God except as the kind of weather people still thank without paperwork.

At the store the fluorescent lights made everyone look slightly guilty. They bought the part the internet swore was right, plus washers the man at the counter insisted on with the authority of experience. Back at Denise’s flat Michael lay on the kitchen floor that smelled of other people’s cooking oil and followed a video with the concentration of prayer. The video host said easy in the way liars and saints both do. Denise made tea too strong and apologised for the mugs not matching.

‘Matching is empire,’ Michael said, then winced. ‘Sorry. Private joke. Mugs are fine. This one is excellent.’

She laughed without needing the joke. They bled radiators, coaxed valves, swore carefully at a nut that had rusted into personality. Heat returned with a rattle of forgiveness. Denise put her palm near the pipe like checking a child’s forehead.

She pressed a tenner on him; he refused; she looked offended in a way that meant dignity; he accepted a jar of jam instead. Homemade. Label in biro: Apricot — D. Bond as jam.

On the stair he paused, jam cold against his wrist. Horizontal beam—God and neighbour, the old cross-geometry from the week before monograms got rude—had always risked being a diagram. Denise was not a diagram. She had a face with lines, a voice with a city in it, a boiler with opinions, a sister named Carol who didn’t call enough.

He texted Joshua a photo of the jam. Horizontal beam has a face. Name: Denise.

Joshua: Write the name down. Names kill abstraction.

R., copied in by accident of group habit: Good. Now help her again when it isn’t photogenic.

Cameron: Also service the friendship before you need a lift yourself. Reciprocity, not hero play.

Michael put the jam on his own shelf like a relic that could be eaten on toast—best kind of relic. Harriet, home late, tasted a spoonful and said, ‘She’s a better theologian than half your notes.’

That weekend he knocked on Denise’s door with surplus soup, not as payment, as continuation. She invited him in for ten minutes of television gossip—someone on a soap had betrayed someone; the audience was supposed to be shocked. He learned Carol’s name properly. He still did not mention α==ω. The boiler hummed like a contented appliance. Praise be to humming.

Walking back down the stairwell that always smelled faintly of other dinners, he understood something filmable and small: the beam becomes real when it risks irritation, time loss, incompetence with tools, the smell of someone else’s life. Abstract love of Neighbour is cheap. Love of Denise costs an evening and grants jam and the right to be asked again.

He added Denise to the ordinary list in his phone under people, not symbols, and set a reminder for nothing mystical—just check in mid-month.

Night settled. Somewhere a boiler kept faith with its repaired part. Michael washed his hands of metal grit and thought of crosses that were death and shame and love—and of how none of that geometry mattered if it never learned a doorbell.

Horizontal beam with a face.

The funny old life, which has always preferred names to diagrams when the chips are down, filed the evening under proof without needing a museum.

Chapter 92

Six And Eight Revisited

846 words

He went back to the keys the way you revisit a place where you almost made a fool of yourself: carefully, with witnesses if possible, and without bringing candles.

Joshua sat on the desk chair that squeaked a confession every time someone shifted weight. Cameron stood with arms folded like a fire marshal of punctuation. R. was on video, face in a small rectangle, tea in hand, bookshelf behind her ordinary and therefore trustworthy. From the kitchen Harriet called, bright as a fuse:

‘If you baptise the keyboard I’m unplugging the router.’

Michael tilted the board into the afternoon light. Dust showed. Six. Eight. Scuffs. An asterisk worn pale from overuse. No conspiracy. Only a laptop that needed cleaning and a brain that had once been high on pattern after a monogram and a metal ballad at 06:08.

‘First visit I almost made moon and star out of hardware,’ he said. He looked at the keys, not at R.’s face; that was where the apology belonged. ‘Red line: not capture. Second visit is inventory. What do these keys mean now—after seal and page and Denise and NRE and a child on my hip?’

Cameron did not soften. Softness was not his job. ‘They mean numbers. Possibly password misery. Possibly the bit of the row you hit when you’re aiming for seven and miss. Humanity. Stop auditioning them for sainthood.’

Joshua leaned forward; the chair squeaked; nobody laughed, because laughter would have made it a show. ‘They mean neighbours on a row. Six learned not to crown itself with seven. Eight waited. Waiting is underrated. Also they mean you still own a laptop that has been through a small war and a biscuit tin.’

R., from the rectangle, raised her tea a fraction. ‘If you must have poetry: six and eight as ordinary time—06:08, 18:31—appointed hours that turned out to be life, not omens. Leave my symbols out of your legends unless I put them there. I am putting them nowhere near your Shift key. Clear?’

‘Clear,’ Michael said. Relief tasted like tea at the right temperature. ‘Appointed hours. Unfaithful at dawn. Harriet at evening. Not destiny. Schedule. The sacredness is showing up, not decoding. The song was irony; the dinner was love; the keys were plastic. Categories held.’

He pressed six. He pressed eight. Click. Click. In the document titled TODO, the cursor made two characters—68—and sat there like a dog that had done a trick and wanted a biscuit, not a theology.

Harriet appeared in the doorway with a tea towel over her shoulder and the expression of a domestic court that had already decided the sentence.

‘Verdict?’

‘Keyboard remains tool,’ Michael said. ‘I remain a man who notices too much and is being trained. The moon and star stay with people who live them. My six and eight are alarms, songs, dinners, the comedy of timestamps. If I ever post a photo of this board with a mystical caption, Cameron may publish my most embarrassing search history.’

‘Logged,’ Cameron said, and almost smiled, which for Cameron was a standing ovation with a risk assessment attached.

‘Acceptable,’ Harriet said, and vanished back toward the kettle, which is how domestic courts adjourn when the evidence is plastic and the defendant has not, for once, made it worse.

R. raised her tea fully. ‘Revisit complete. Close the ritual before it becomes one. I’m hanging up before someone lights a candle.’

She left the call. The rectangle went black. Joshua squeezed Michael’s shoulder once, firm, brotherly, no sermon.

‘You looked. You left. That’s the whole spiritual practice for some Tuesdays.’

They moved to the table for actual food—bread, cheese, Denise’s jam, which Harriet served without announcing symbolism. Conversation turned to work gossip and a film with a plot hole the size of a museum map; Joshua was offended on behalf of lifts. Six and eight did not come up again. The absence was the success.

After the flat quieted—Cameron gone, Joshua gone with leftover bread, Harriet reading on the sofa—Michael stood once more at the desk without opening the laptop.

He could still feel the itch: the mind’s wish to make a chain link out of every rhyme, every scuff, every number that had ever sat beside another number and looked, for one heady second, like a message from the management.

He let the itch be an itch. He did not scratch it into a claim. He wiped the dust from six and eight with his sleeve, which is not a sacrament, and walked away before the sleeve could get ideas.

In the living room Harriet turned a page without looking up. ‘Still a tool?’

‘Still a tool.’

‘Good. There’s a password for the router on a sticky note if you forget how numbers work in ordinary life.’

Michael sat. The itch faded into the hum of a flat that contained people and cheese and a keyboard allowed to remain plastic—

—and he slept that night without dreaming of fonts, which felt less like loss than like a small, quiet victory scored in the only court that mattered.

Chapter 93

Islam In The Chain Without Capture

758 words

The question was whether Islam could appear in Michael’s chain at all without becoming a prisoner of it.

He asked it out loud in the interfaith centre’s kitchen, washing cups after Scripture Reasoning, R. drying, Joshua stacking, Cameron having left early with a salute and a text that said don’t crown the dishwasher.

Soap bubbles held small distorted rooms. Michael watched one pop and thought: that’s capture—force a whole house into a film that bursts when you touch it.

‘I have a chain,’ he said. ‘Experience, unity, crossbeams, both/and, monogram, no-sin-as-outside, funny old life. Then Act IV: you, seal, think, respect. If I draw the chain on a page, do I automatically capture you by inclusion? Is the diagram already a cage?’

R. dried slower, tea towel methodical. ‘Depends how you draw. If Islam is a bead you strung to complete your necklace—capture. If Islam is a neighbour whose house you refuse to annex while admitting you live on the same street—maybe not. Necklaces are for wearers. Streets are for people who go home to different doors.’

Joshua stacked a cup with a clink of ordinary liturgy. ‘Chain as sequence of my learning, not as ontology of the world. First person. I learned X. I met R. I was corrected. That’s memoir ethics. Universal diagram ethics are different and dirtier. Memoir can love a neighbour. Diagrams try to own the postcode.’

Michael rinsed until the water ran clear. ‘So the chapter title is a warning label. Islam in the chain without capture means: presence without ownership. Influence without rebrand. Citation without trophy. Exit rights in the fine print.’

‘Exit rights,’ R. said. ‘I can leave your story. I’m not a permanent exhibit in the Michael Museum of Growth. If you write me as the Muslim Friend Who Proves Universality, I will haunt your footnotes.’

‘Exit rights,’ he repeated. Bond language. He almost crossed his heart and didn’t, because gesture without content is another soft theft.

They finished the cups. The facilitator waved from the doorway; lights dimmed section by section; they stepped into night air that smelled of rain on warm pavement. A takeaway bag skittered past like a low-budget tumbleweed.

Walking, Michael tried a careful sentence, counting it on his fingers so he wouldn’t add a quiet annex. ‘What I take, with credit: the demand to think; the seriousness of seal as protection; the lived discipline of prayer I don’t perform and won’t cosplay; the refusal to be halved into heart or mind; your face when I almost used you as proof—that face is in the chain as correction. What I don’t take: the right to say what Islam is for Muslims; the moon and star as my alphabet; finality as a costume for my open page; your name as a badge on my coat.’

R. was quiet long enough that he feared he had failed. A car passed, throwing light across her expression—thinking, not performing kindness. Then: ‘That list is acceptable. Publish it in your bones. If you write the book one day, I get to read the R. pages before strangers do. Bond. And if I say cut, you cut.’

‘Bond,’ Michael said. ‘Cut means cut.’

Joshua exhaled a long breath that might have been a laugh. ‘I like this street. Houses different. Pavement shared. No one pretending the architecture is identical. That’s the bigger book’s zoning law.’

Cameron texted as if he had bugged the pavement: If your chain diagram has a little crescent “owned by plot,” redraw. Also thank R. with food not theology.

Michael: Redrawing as memoir not monopoly. Food idea noted.

At home he opened the notebook to the long spine-paragraph habit of the bad drafts—the tour of every station in one indigestible paragraph—and crossed out the impulse to summarise the world. He wrote instead, slowly:

Chain = what happened to me.
Islam = among the realities that corrected me.
Correction ≠ possession.
R. = friend with exit rights, not a station.

Harriet read it over his shoulder, chin on his shoulder-bone. ‘Frame that. Not on the wall—on your manners. And buy her lunch next week without an agenda.’

‘Agenda-free lunch,’ Michael said. ‘The hardest ritual.’

He closed the book. Islam in the chain without capture was not a clever knot.

It was a way of walking past a house you do not own, grateful for the light in the window, refusing to move the furniture with your eyes—

—and the funny old life, which has more houses than any one man can map, let that be a kind of peace.

Chapter 94

The Bigger Book

698 words

They met to name the thing properly—not as argument, as title.

Café, window table, rain performing its old play against glass that needed cleaning. R. with her notebook open to a blank page on purpose. Michael with empty hands. Joshua present as midwife. Cameron present as the man who would say forty-two if the poetry swelled, already unwrapping a sugar packet like a fuse.

‘I wanted a bigger book,’ Michael said. ‘After the monogram. After the last line. I thought bigger meant more pages of me being right in new directions. It’s come to mean more room for people who aren’t my directions. Hospitality, not annexation. If that’s still ego in a nicer coat, tell me now.’

R. drew an open rectangle, not a circle. ‘Bigger book as hospitality. Not as sequel that eats prior volumes. Circles close. Rectangles leave a side for someone to enter. I’m fond of rectangles today.’

Joshua smiled into his cup. ‘Canon as library, not as single scroll with your monogram as binding. Libraries let you leave with different books. Scrolls want to be the only meal.’

Cameron stabbed the sugar packet once, gently. ‘Define success so we can audit you. Vague success is how cults file taxes.’

Michael thought of Denise’s jam, Harriet’s plates, soft play knees, red lines said out loud, NRE cooling into bin night, seal and page distinguished so they stopped bruising each other, a colleague’s boat with a hole, a keyboard left unbaptised.

‘Success,’ he said, counting on fingers where Cameron could see: ‘I keep learning without treating friends as chapters I control. I hold Christ == God without making R. a failed equation. I hold no-outside without erasing different boats. I keep writing—living—without demanding a publisher called Destiny. I repair when I break a word. I go home and wash up.’

R. added, pen moving: ‘Success on my side: I remain unannexed and still invited. I correct you without becoming your conscience-for-hire. I keep my seal without needing you to hate your open page. I eat with you after hard sentences.’

They sat with the mutual contract. The barista called a name that wasn’t theirs; someone else’s story collected a latte and left a tip that was either generous or guilty. Rain thickened. The window became a soft wall.

Joshua raised his cup. ‘The bigger book is already being written. It’s the week. It’s the washing-up. It’s Scripture Reasoning without a winner. It’s Cameron leaving early. It’s Harriet at 18:31. It’s Denise’s boiler. You don’t finish it. You inhabit it. Finishing is the capstone urge again.’

Michael felt the old hunger ease into something like vocation without badge. ‘Final chapter and bigger book—different objects. We said it over pasta. I feel it now in my bones. Her finality doesn’t close my homework. My homework doesn’t reopen her seal as if it were a zip file.’

‘Zip file,’ Cameron said. ‘Please never say that in a sermon. Or a café. Or near me.’

‘I’m not giving sermons,’ Michael said. ‘I’m buying the next round of tea. That is my missionary method for the next ten minutes.’

He did. When he returned, careful with hot cardboard, R. had written on a napkin—danger stationery reclaimed—BIGGER BOOK = MORE ROOM, NOT MORE THRONE.

Michael pocketed the napkin beside the monogram one. Two napkins. No merger. Wallet getting thick with ethics, thin with cash—as it should be.

Outside, under shared umbrellas that were not metaphors unless you were weak, they parted at the corner where the bus threw water at their shoes. R. said, ‘Title accepted. Live it. Don’t brand it.’ Joshua said amen. Cameron said forty-two and meant don’t get weird in the group chat.

Michael walked the long way, past the canal, and did not open the notebook. He let the city write a few lines: bus hiss, wet leaves, a child in yellow wellies stamping a puddle into empire and then laughing it away. The bigger book had no last page he could own.

It had this pavement, these friends, this refusal to finish other people—

—and the funny old life, which had always been bigger than one climax, turned another damp page without asking him to crown it.

Chapter 95

Missionary Without Eggshells

796 words

‘I don’t want to be a missionary,’ Michael said.

‘Good,’ said R.

‘I also don’t want to walk on eggshells until I become a ghost who never says what he loves.’

‘Also good,’ said R. ‘Harder. Ghosts are popular at parties. They never spill wine on doctrine.’

They were in the bookshop from the old moon-and-star window week—different display now, same bell above the door, same smell of paper and glue and people pretending not to judge each other’s covers. Joshua browsed nearby as emotional cover, holding a poetry book he would not buy. Cameron had refused to enter a bookshop on principle of surplus unread piles and sent a text instead: If you convert anyone in Soft Furnishings I revoke chip privileges.

R. pulled a chair in the tiny café at the back. The table wobbled; Michael folded a receipt under the leg—practical theology. ‘Eggshells are what people do when they fear conflict more than falsehood,’ she said. ‘They smile, they dilute, they call it respect. It’s often just anxiety management. Real respect can bear weight. It can hear Christ == God and say not for me without ending the meal. It can hear my seal and not rebrand it as your open page.’

Michael turned a sugar sachet end over end. ‘And missionary is what people do when they fear difference more than violence. They love you toward their map until you disappear. How do I speak from love without hunting? I can feel the hunt in my throat when I get excited. Excitement is not permission.’

Joshua dropped into a chair with the poetry book as prop. ‘Consent. Context. Exit ramps. You share because asked or because friendship has earned a turn—not because silence burns your ego. You stop when the other says stop. You don’t punish stop with coldness. Coldness after no is just missionary work with the heater off.’

R. nodded, tearing a corner off a napkin without writing on it—progress. ‘Missionary without eggshells means: clear speech, low coercion, high warmth, no sulk. You may invite. You may not corner. You may care. You may not collect. If you leave a conversation needing a win, you were hunting. If you leave needing to have been kind, you might have been a friend.’

Michael practised a sentence, quiet enough for the café, firm enough to be real. ‘I think the monogram is true the way jokes are true—recognition, not billyclub. I live under Christ as God. I think you are whole without becoming me. I would like us to keep arguing kindly. Full stop. No metric on your enthusiasm.’

R. cocked her head, testing for hooks. ‘I’d receive that. I would not receive a follow-up that measured my salvation by my smile at your napkin. No follow-up metrics. Bond.’

‘No follow-up metrics,’ Michael said. ‘Bond.’

A bookseller wheeled a trolley past; new hardbacks gleamed like recruits. Michael felt the old urge to put his book among them as weapon and let it pass like a bus he did not need. Joshua returned the poetry book to the shelf with the tenderness of men who know unread piles are also ethics.

Cameron texted again: If you need to win the silent argument in your head after, you were still hunting. Notice. Then go home.

Michael showed them. Joshua said, ‘Sandpaper gospel. Blessed are the unimpressed.’

They left with no new comparative religion purchases—Michael’s choice, a small fast from acquisition. On the pavement he asked, ‘If someone earnest asks me what I believe at a party, eggshells or mission?’

‘Honesty with volume control,’ R. said. ‘Three sentences. Then questions about them. If they lean in, continue. If they talk about football, play football. God is not always the only ball. Sometimes the ball is actual football. Touch grass. Touch the horizontal beam. Touch the crisp bowl.’

Michael laughed. ‘That’s the most pastoral thing you’ve said.’

‘Don’t make me a pastor in your chain,’ she said, smiling. ‘Make me a friend who tells you when your volume is for you, not for truth. Volume-for-you is eggshells’ loud cousin—still about your comfort.’

Home, he wrote the rules on paper and stuck them inside the notebook cover where he would see them before he saw his own cleverness:

Speak clear.
Don’t corner.
No sulk after no.
Warmth ≠ win.
Football allowed.
Three sentences, then questions.

Harriet read them over his shoulder. ‘Finally. A missionary position I can live with.’

He groaned. She grinned, unrepentant, and kissed the place where sulking lives.

Missionary without eggshells: adult speech in a world that swings between shout and mute.

The funny old life, which includes parties and football and napkin jokes that are not billyclubs, left room for his voice—

—and room for everyone else’s silence without calling either a war.

Chapter 96

Blasphemy Shaped Like Love

775 words

The monogram still offended people. That was data, not a marketing plan.

Michael discovered it again when a man at a dinner—friend of a friend, confident wine, the sort of confidence that owns a holiday home in his opinions—heard a careful version of α==ω and went grey, then loud. ‘That’s blasphemy. You’re sexualising God.’

The table froze in the British way: eyes on roast potatoes, cutlery paused mid-air as if etiquette could reverse time. Someone coughed into a napkin. The host smiled the smile of people who regret their guest list.

Michael set his fork down. Heart rate up. NRE ghost reached for a microphone and a martyr’s halo. He breathed. He saw R. across the candles, not a prop—a friend with her own red lines. He saw Joshua’s small nod: careful, not cowardly.

‘It might be blasphemy in your court,’ he said. ‘In mine it’s a claim that generation isn’t a dirty annex God outsources to a shame department. I’m not asking you to print it on a youth-group hoodie. I’m saying the joke landed as love for me—love of bodies, love of the whole, love that refuses a pure upstairs where sex is other people’s problem. If that still sounds like filth only, we may be stuck. I’ll live with stuck. I won’t live with sneering at your grandmother to feel free.’

‘Love,’ the man said, sceptical as a closed gate. ‘Convenient word.’

R., present because the host collected interesting combinations of humans the way some people collect cookbooks, spoke before Michael could dig a cleverer hole. ‘Some blasphemy is just cruelty wearing a mask of freedom. Some “blasphemy” is tenderness that refuses a cruel purity. Discernment. I don’t share his monogram. I have watched it make him less contemptuous of flesh, including his own. That shape I will call love-adjacent. If it makes him sneer at your grandmother’s piety, it has flipped into something else—and I will say so to his face, not behind a potato.’

Joshua, across the table, nodded. ‘Shame into love was my family’s homework. If the scandal doesn’t produce care, it’s cosplay. Cosplay with incense is still cosplay.’

The man still disliked it. He was allowed. Dislike is not a crime; contempt can be. Dinner continued with a heroic pivot to weather, then to a football score that let everyone breathe. Michael passed the gravy. Gravy is underrated diplomacy.

Later, washing up with the host’s too-small towel in a kitchen that smelled of fat and relief, Michael said to R. and Joshua in the steam: ‘I needed that phrase. Blasphemy shaped like love. Also: love shaped like blasphemy to systems that need disgust to run. I don’t want to become a professional offender. I also don’t want to sand the joke into a pebble that never cuts anything false.’

R. handed him a plate. ‘Don’t fall in love with being offensive. That’s ego’s favourite coat—looks brave, warms only you. Fall in love with being accurate and kind at the same time. If accuracy looks like scandal, bear the cost without recruiting martyrs. Martyrs at dinner parties are exhausting.’

Cameron, informed by text while Michael dried, replied: If your “love” needs a victim to feel real, it’s not love. If their “reverence” needs a scapegoat body to feel clean, it’s not reverence. Both fail. Potatoes are innocent. Forty-two.

Michael showed the others. Steam curled around the phone like stage smoke, unearned. Joshua snorted. ‘Cameron just saved Christianity and decency in one text. Don’t tell him.’

Walking home, Harriet linked her arm through his. Streetlights made wet pavement into cheap glitter. ‘You didn’t perform. You also didn’t collapse. Proud. Now tell me privately if you’re okay—not the theology, the body.’

He was shaky and clear. Adrenaline leaving like a bad guest. ‘I keep wanting everyone to laugh with the napkin. Some people will always hear only filth. I can’t control their ears. I can control whether I use the scandal to feel superior. Superior is the real blasphemy if anything is—standing outside the only room sneering.’

‘That last bit,’ Harriet said. ‘Tattoo it on your manners. Not on your skin. Manners last longer if you practise.’

At home he took out both napkins—monogram and bigger-book—and did not add a third. He sat on the bed edge until the adrenaline left, Harriet’s hand on his back, no speech required.

Blasphemy shaped like love: possible.

Love shaped like a weapon: also possible.

The work was telling them apart at dinner, with potatoes listening—

—and the funny old life, which has always known that holy and shocking sometimes share a coat, asked only that the coat keep someone warm without picking their pocket.

Chapter 97

The Capstone Problem

733 words

The floating tip returned in a dream: Michael placing a little pyramid on a big one while friends’ faces flattened into bricks under his hands. He woke sour, mouth dry, and went for a run that punished the image into sweat and pavement. A fox watched him from a garden like a critic. He did not ask the fox for a blurb.

At breakfast he told Harriet. She buttered toast with judicial calm. ‘Capstone problem: who finishes the shape, who claims the view, who gets buried in the base. You’ve been demoting yourself from peak for weeks. Good. Don’t sneak back up via “I’m the man who refuses peaks.” That’s a peak with better PR. Anti-peak is still a brand if you sell it.’

He winced into coffee. ‘Caught. The dream was a performance review.’

Joshua met him at the canal with two coffees and no children’s Egypt book this time—advanced level, adult mud. Swans conducted foreign policy near the bank.

‘Capstone problem is structural,’ Joshua said. ‘Movements want finishers. Brands want logos on the tip. You want your recognition to matter without becoming the tip of other people’s lives. So: refuse the job whenever it’s offered—including by your own mirror. Especially by your own mirror. Mirrors hire badly.’

Michael watched a swan hiss at a tourist who had believed in bread. ‘R.’s seal looks like a capstone to my old eyes. I’ve learned it’s community protection, different object. My monogram looks like a capstone to the dinner man. To me it’s a joke in a wallet. Perspective is a hall of tips. Everyone thinks someone else’s completion is arrogance.’

‘Then don’t trust your felt sense of “I’m only being humble,”’ Joshua said. ‘Trust behaviour. Do you interrupt more or less? Do you need credit? Do you vanish when you’re not the interesting one? Do you let Cameron have the last word without a both/and encore?’

Michael ran an honest audit while coffee cooled and disliked some line items. He sent apologies that morning—two short messages for two small throne-grabs in group chats. Cost: mild embarrassment. Benefit: spine. One person replied no worries; the other did not reply, which was also data.

R. joined them on a bench, free for twenty minutes, scarf against wind. ‘Capstone problem in interfaith: everyone wants to be the final interpreter of everyone else. Solution: don’t. Be a brick. Bricks are allowed to be beautiful. They’re not allowed to pretend they’re the sky. Sky doesn’t need your CV.’

Cameron, remote as weather: Also: unfinished pyramids are honest. The gap you drew is a feature. Live in the draft. Drafts don’t get statues. Good.

Michael laughed, surprising himself. ‘Draft theology. I can live there. Statues collect pigeon opinions anyway.’

‘All theology is draft if you’re not dead,’ R. said. ‘And even then, people edit you. Try not to make their job easy by being a tyrant in life.’

They sat until her timer buzzed. Before she left she poked Michael’s shoulder. ‘If you ever publish, no cover with you on a mountain. Cover with a table. Or jam. Or a keyboard that stays a keyboard. Or potatoes from the dinner where you didn’t win.’

‘Jam,’ Michael said solemnly. ‘Denise would approve. Harriet would demand royalties for the toast.’

Alone later, he sketched a pyramid and put an X through the tip, not as vandalism of traditions that need their own completions, but as a personal restraining order. He wrote under it:

I am not the capstone.
I am not the anti-capstone saint either.
I am a man in a draft, carrying plates.

Evening, he helped Harriet with a flat-pack shelf—the most anti-peak activity available this side of laundry. Instructions in five languages. One missing screw. Laughter that bent them double. A shelf that held books without becoming a metaphor until Michael almost made it one and Harriet said, ‘Don’t. Let it be a shelf. The books can be pretentious without the furniture joining in.’

He let it be a shelf. He put novels on it, and a cookbook, and nothing that claimed to finish God.

The capstone problem did not vanish. Problems that live in prestige never vanish; they apply for promotions under new names.

They get outvoted by jam, shelves, and friends who refuse to be bricks in your dream—

—and the funny old life, unfinished on purpose, left the tip off so the air could still move.

Chapter 98

Almost Morning

900 words

The sky went from black to a colour that insisted it was not black anymore, and Michael was awake for it without having planned a vigil.

Almost morning. The house ticked. A pipe somewhere made a remark in the language of houses that have opinions about temperature. Harriet breathed the slow rhythm of people who have decided sleep is not a moral failure. On the chair by the door, his jacket held the wallet like a pocketed library: monogram napkin, bigger-book napkin, a receipt from soup night, a loyalty card for coffee he kept forgetting to stamp.

He stood at the window with tea and watched the street become itself. Bins at slight angles, as if they had argued and settled. A fox with a freelance career crossed the road without asking for a blurb. The first bus arrived with its cargo of early duty—people whose faces had not yet put on the day, only the coat. A streetlight hesitated, then admitted the sky was winning, and went out with the dignity of a minor official clocking off.

Almost morning was a tense that suited him. Not the climax of chapter sixty-five—napkin, pub, final line spoken into a room that had not asked for a sermon. Not yet the full daylight of toast and the reprise he could feel waiting like a train three stops away. The in-between: recognition sharing a body with fatigue, unpaid emails, desire, love that snored lightly in the next room, a hip that still complained if he pretended he was made of ideas.

He sipped tea gone lukewarm and did not mind. Lukewarm was honest. Joshua would still be asleep. Cameron would call early hours a personal attack and mean it. R. might already be awake in her own rhythm; Michael did not narrate that into his private film. He sent no texts. Almost morning can be ruined by performing it for an audience of phones.

A delivery van reversed with the same beep as on the first morning after recognition. Continuity as comedy. The universe, if it had a sense of humour—and Michael still thought it did—was not above using health-and-safety audio as a punchline.

He took the wallet from the jacket, removed the monogram napkin, and looked at it without electricity. α==ω. Dual joke. Generative claim. Still true. Also: paper, fold-worn, a bit ridiculous, survivable without empire and without a camera. He smoothed one corner that wanted to curl, the way paper does when it has been loved too hard and carried too long.

‘Almost morning,’ he told the glass. ‘We’re still here.’

The glass did not argue. Experience existed. The field had no outside. The neighbours would need kindness that was not a diagram. Denise’s boiler would fail again someday. He would go again if he could, jam or no jam, photo or no photo.

He put the napkin back. The wallet closed with a soft sound like a small book deciding not to make a scene.

In the kitchen he rinsed the mug and left it on the draining board—draft theology in crockery form. He sliced bread for toast and set the dial where it would not burn if he paid attention. Paying attention was the whole religion in work clothes: not the absence of God, not the presence of a press release, just the refusal to outsource the next hour to symbols while the bread blackened.

Outside, the fox vanished into a hedge with the professionalism of creatures who do not need last lines. A cyclist went past with a light slightly too bright, apologising to the dark by dazzling it. Michael watched until the street was only a street again.

He sat on the sofa and closed his eyes for twenty minutes of not-sleep, a soft buffer between night and day. Almost morning held him like a waiting room that was also the appointment. He did not inventory the bigger book. Inventory was yesterday’s temptation. Today’s work was simpler and harder: stay in the body that had to make coffee for two, say something unimpressive—sleep okay?—and mean it when Harriet’s alarm rang.

Her phone would chirp in an hour. He would not wake her early to share the sky. Shared sky is better when both people choose it. He had learned that the expensive way, with intensity seasons and the look she got when he treated dawn like a product launch.

A bird attempted a song and failed, tried again, succeeded in a thin way that still counted. Michael opened his eyes. The room was the room: sofa, chair, jacket, the faint smell of last night’s washing-up liquid. No vision. No metal ballad. No need.

He stood, stretched the hip, and put the kettle on for the second time—not as ritual, as thirst. When the water boiled he made coffee strong enough to survive conversation and left Harriet’s mug waiting beside the pot like a promise that did not require Greek.

Almost morning ended the way good thresholds do: without a drumroll. Light filled the kitchen corner by corner. Somewhere a bus took the early duty people toward their lives. Michael buttered nothing yet. He waited for the alarm, for the unimpressive question, for the day that would not ask him to caption it—

—and when Harriet’s phone finally sang, he was already pouring, already present, already almost ready to be fully here without needing the night to clap.

Chapter 99

It's A Funny Old Life (Reprise)

807 words

Morning arrived properly, with toast smoke and a busker somewhere practising optimism on a corner that did not tip well.

This was not chapter sixty-five. That landing stayed landed: the pub, the empty chair, the napkin, the dual reading, the line spoken as recognition’s first full breath. No one was rewriting the end of the monogram week. The monogram still lived in the wallet, fold-worn, slightly ridiculous, still true.

This was the morning after living it—weeks of bigger book, red lines, boilers, biscuits, seals, pages, soft play, dinner-table scandal, shelves that remained shelves, foxes in hedges, jam that was not a metaphor until someone weak made it one.

Michael buttered toast for Harriet. One edge had caught; he scraped the bitter off without philosophy. She hummed something off-key and stole a corner of his crust. Joshua knocked and entered with the key and a bag of apples again, as if fibre were a sacrament. Cameron arrived ten minutes later, complaining about the hour and staying anyway, shoes off by the door in the old Friday-night way. R. came last, scarf, smile, no trophy light in anyone’s eyes.

They fit around the table because the table had practised. Elbows negotiated. The cat of temporary custody watched from a chair like a minor official.

‘Status report,’ Cameron said, pouring coffee. ‘Anyone founded a cult?’

‘No,’ Michael said.

‘Annexed a friend?’ R. asked lightly.

‘No,’ Michael said.

‘Crowned a keyboard?’ Joshua asked.

‘No,’ Harriet said for him, firm.

Michael laughed, and the laugh had sleep in it, which made it honest. ‘I am a man who still thinks α==ω is true, Christ == God is true, no-outside is true, and Denise’s jam is also true. I am trying to keep those truths from eating each other.’

R. took an apple, turned it, found a bruise, ate around the bruise without speechifying. ‘Acceptable status. Friendship continues.’

Joshua rolled an apple to Cameron. ‘Amen to continues.’

Cameron caught it one-handed. ‘Forty-two. And washing-up. In that order if the universe forces a choice.’

They ate. They talked about nothing cosmic: a TV show that had wasted a good premise, a work irritation with a name, a plan to fix Denise’s shelf next Saturday, a Scripture Reasoning date that was optional and open. The monogram did not get a speaking role. It didn’t need one. It had become part of the furniture of Michael’s mind—like a window, not a throne. Light came through. You did not stand on it to address the nation.

When the plates stacked, Michael stood at the sink and felt the cold floor through socks. First experiment, last experiment: he was here. They were here. The room had no pure elsewhere to dump love or harm into. Steam rose from the tap. Someone’s phone buzzed and was ignored—small modern piety.

Harriet leaned on the counter beside him. ‘You’re quiet.’

‘I’m noticing without a press release,’ he said. ‘It turns out that’s harder than revelation.’

‘Do it anyway. And pass the sponge.’

R. washed; Joshua dried; Cameron supervised with commentary about tea-towel technique; Michael put away. The choreography was clumsy and kind. A glass nearly died; Joshua saved it; Cameron claimed credit; R. rolled her eyes in three religions’ worth of fondness.

At the door, goodbyes stacked like blessings that refused to be formal. Cameron left first—before anyone sings. R. squeezed Michael’s arm once: red lines still green. Joshua hugged him with brother-force and left two apples on purpose. Harriet closed the door and leaned her forehead on Michael’s for a second of private horizontal beam.

Alone in the quiet kitchen, Michael opened the wallet, saw the two napkins, and left them there. He did not read them aloud. He did not need to. The busker outside hit a wrong note and laughed at himself; the street forgave him.

Michael said the line to the window, to the field, to you—not as a discovery now, as a practice, as weather reported by a man with jam on the shelf and friends who leave early and stay when it counts:

The sky did not write it on a cloud. The toast had burned slightly on one edge. He ate what remained anyway.

Ontology survived.

So did kindness, which had always been the point under the joke.

He thought of the first cold morning, of Unfaithful at 06:08, of the ridge wind, of a child on his hip, of a boiler returning to life, of R. saying guest not ingredient, of Cameron going to bed, of Joshua saying amen over chips. The chain was not a cage. It was a path he had walked with company.

Michael washed the last mug, set it down, and let the day take him—emails, love, preventable horrors, tea—

—with the only line that could carry climax and aftermath without lying about either:

It's a funny old life.
